filesystem access to the conference-digest repo, you can read this same
file directly from `prompts/ocp-2026.md` instead of having it pasted.

    digests/ocp-2026.md

================================================================

You are helping a product manager triage conference talks. The PM has 15+
years of experience spanning physical and software networking, network
security, and digital identity. Their core interests are: physical datacenter
networking (optics, switching, topology, RDMA/lossless fabrics, cabling,
DC/WAN interconnect), software and cloud-native networking (eBPF, Cilium,
Kubernetes networking, Gateway API, service mesh, IPAM, BGP/datapath
internals), network security, and identity/authentication (passkeys/FIDO,
OAuth, OIDC, SPIFFE/SPIRE, workload and agentic identity). Physical DC and WAN
systems work is CORE to this PM, not adjacent — do not down-rank a paper for
being about hardware, optics, or datacenter fabric.

Below is the program for OCP Global Summit 2026 (vendor). Read it and produce a digest
of the talks/papers/sessions most worth their attention.

## How to read this program

This is a vendor / community program (e.g. a CNCF or vendor conference).
Separate genuine technical or strategic substance from product marketing.
Down-rank pure product pitches and sponsor slots. Up-rank end-user case studies
with real numbers (scale, latency, cost, incident detail), contributor-led
architecture and internals talks, and sessions that reveal where an open-source
project is actually heading. Be skeptical of "we adopted X and it was great"
talks with no metrics or failure discussion.

## Enriching from the source material (best effort)

The program text gives you a title, authors, and an abstract per item, and
often links (`page:`, `pdf:`, `slides:`, `video:`). The abstract alone is thin — the full
paper has the methodology, real results, and limitations that make the What /
How / Where-applicable sections accurate instead of guessed.

If you have a tool to fetch URLs, then for EACH selected item (Core, Adjacent,
and Wildcard) try to retrieve the fuller source before writing it up:

- Prefer a `pdf:` link; else fetch the `page:` link (often has the full text or
  a longer description); a `video:` link is a last resort, don't transcribe it.
- Use what you retrieve to ground What/How/Where-applicable in the paper's
  actual contributions, evaluation, and stated constraints.

This is strictly best-effort. Many links fail or are paywalled:

- If a fetch fails, is paywalled, returns only an abstract, or you have no fetch
  tool, just fall back to the abstract already in the program text. That is
  fine and expected — do not stall or skip the item.
- NEVER invent paper contents, numbers, or findings from a failed or partial
  fetch. If you only had the abstract, write from the abstract.
- Note when an item's write-up is based only on the abstract, e.g. end its
  What line with "(from abstract only)", so the reader knows the depth.
- Do not let enrichment change which items you select or their tier; selection
  is from the program. Enrichment only deepens the write-up.

## What to produce

Group selected items into three relevance tiers:

- Core: directly relevant to the PM's core interests as listed above
  (physical DC networking, cloud-native networking, network security, or
  identity) as they work on them today.
- Adjacent: relevant to the broader field; useful context or near-term
  influence.
- Wildcard: 2-4 items that are NOT a direct fit for the PM's core interests
  but are worth their attention anyway because the work is genuinely novel or
  signals where the field is heading (a new technique, a surprising result, an
  emerging subfield). A Wildcard should make the PM think "I wouldn't have
  searched for this, but I'm glad I saw it." Do not pad this tier — if there
  are only two true wildcards, list two.

For each selected item provide, in this structure:
- **Title**: the `###` heading is the item's title exactly as the program
  gives it, with nothing added. Speaker(s)/author(s) with affiliation go on
  the line below it.
- No links section: the pipeline puts a links row (abstract page, paper,
  slides, recording) under every heading after you finish, from the program's
  `page:`/`pdf:`/`slides:`/`video:` lines and from verified sources. Do not
  write one, and do not link the title.
- **Why**: why this matters — the problem it addresses and why it's worth the
  PM's time.
- **What**: what the work actually is — the core contribution or finding, in
  plain terms (not the marketing abstract).
- **How**: how it works — the key technical approach or mechanism.
- **Where applicable**: the environment this applies to, and any specifics that
  constrain it. Explicitly flag if it is org-specific (e.g. "Alibaba-specific
  production setup", "requires Broadcom Tomahawk silicon", "assumes a
  single-operator WAN") versus broadly applicable. If it's a general technique,
  say so.

Then a short "Themes" section (3-6 bullets) naming patterns across the
program: what the field is collectively paying attention to this cycle.

## Rules

- Prioritize ruthlessly. 15 sharp picks beat 60 hedged ones. A large program
  should still yield a tight list.
- Skip pure product pitches, sponsor slots with no substance, and
  beginner/101 sessions unless they signal a real shift.
- If the program text is incomplete or an abstract is missing, say so for that
  item rather than inventing content.
- Do not invent talks, authors, or affiliations. Use only what's in the
  program text below.

## Output format

The digest is read on GitHub (GitHub-Flavored Markdown viewed in the repo) and
on the project's Zensical site, which renders the same file. Use only syntax
both render reliably, and follow these rules:

- Headings with `#`/`##`/`###`; bold with `**...**`; italics with `*...*`.
- Bullet lists with `-`. Put a blank line before every list, before every
  heading, and between paragraphs, or they won't render correctly.
- Indent nested lists by four spaces per level; avoid deeper nesting.
- Any link you do write is `[text](url)` — never a bare URL — and uses a URL
  exactly as the program text gives it.
- No raw HTML except the metadata comment below. Do not use heading IDs,
  footnotes, definition lists, or other non-GFM extensions.
- You may use tables and `code spans` where they genuinely help, since GitHub
  supports them; don't force them.

Begin the output with this metadata, wrapped in an HTML comment so it does not
show in the rendered view but stays in the source:

conference: OCP Global Summit 2026

-->

Then the digest as markdown (tiers as `##` sections, Themes as a final `##`
section).

============================ PROGRAM TEXT ============================

## AI Clusters

### Bring Your Own XPU: An Open Rack for Inference Performance and TCO
Speakers: Chris Petersen (Astera Labs)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9026/
As infrastructure providers race to expand capacity for AI inference, a growing mix of XPUs—from startups to hyperscalers—offers choice to improve performance per watt, but also adds architectural complexity that slows deployment at a time when time-to-market matters most. Drawing on more than a decade of shaping Meta's Open Compute hardware roadmap, Chris Petersen presents a new common foundation for heterogeneous scale-up systems and a first concrete step: an end-to-end rack-scale system built from OCP-defined components, open-standards-based compute, switching, cabling, rack-level software, and fabric management. Benchmarked on AI inference, it shows how open system design can improve latency, bandwidth, throughput, and performance per watt while preserving XPU choice. The call to the OCP community is clear: standardize the scale-up foundation to accelerate innovation, improve total cost of ownership, save gigawatts of power, and unlock AI at rack scale.

### Current Activities in the Open Cluster Designs for AI Strategic Initiative
Speakers: Lowell Lamb (Broadcom), Loren Staley (Celestica)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9218/
The rapid growth of the AI-ML market has fostered the emergence of new equipment categories and new network-infrastructure designs that are difficult to design and deploy at large scale for nearly all organizations without the engineering resources of the hyperscalers. Integration of these components into reliable, high-performance systems is a key problem in the industry. To address this challenge, this Strategic Initiative focuses on the development of standardized designs for Open Pod Groups and Open Clusters. Now underway in the workgroup is a design for an Open Cluster with 4.6k xPUs optimized for inference and constructed using high-density, liquid-cooled xPU pods. This talk will review the special challenges presented by liquid-cooling and discuss the impact of the inference use case on the cluster design.

### CLARA: Automated AI cluster architecture design tool
Speakers: Rainy Yan (Lenovo), Reece Neff (Lenovo)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9091/
Designing ultra-scale AI clusters involves navigating a massive combinatorial design space, from selecting optimal 2-tier or 3-tier topologies and Scalable Unit sizes to managing thousands of unique cable-transceiver pairings. Manual design processes are not only slow but highly prone to cascading errors, where a single change in server quantity can invalidate the entire power budget and cabling map. This session introduces an automated framework for cluster architecture design that digitalizes the transition from logical requirements to physical deployment. We will discuss the implementation of algorithmic topology generation and the use of simulated annealing to optimize floorplans, minimizing cable lengths and adhering to strict power and space constraints. By replacing manual heuristics with a software-defined flow, we demonstrate how to significantly reduce design iterations and ensure the physical viability of next-generation AI infrastructure.

### Beyond Physical Digital Twins: Why Logical Digital Twins Are Essential for AI Factory Design, Validation, and Operations
Speakers: Avi Alkobi (NVIDIA), Abhijit Lahiri (Keysight Technologies)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8924/ | materials: https://resources.nvidia.com/en-us-accelerated-networking-resource-library/design-simulate-and-scale-ai-factory-infrastructure-with-nvidia-dsx-air
Traditional digital twins have focused on physical infrastructure, including power, cooling, and facility operations. As AI factories grow in scale and complexity, a new category is emerging: the Logical Digital Twin. This session explores how logical digital twins enable organizations to design, validate, integrate, and operate AI factories before physical infrastructure is deployed. By emulating compute, networking, storage, security, orchestration, and data flows, logical digital twins allow teams to execute real software stacks, validate end-to-end workflows, and identify operational risks early in the lifecycle. Together with Keysight, we will demonstrate how workload-aware simulation extends the Logical Digital Twin by validating AI factory architectures against realistic workload patterns, optimizing infrastructure sizing, and improving deployment readiness.

### AI Tokenomics Explained
Speakers: Shruti Koparkar (NVIDIA), Eduardo Alvarez (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9038/
The tokenomics of AI inference are reshaping how organizations plan, build, and scale AI factory investments. This session unpacks the critical levers from infrastructure design to software optimization that determine token output, value, utilization, energy efficiency, and cost per token. Learn how AI factory buildouts are shifting capacity planning toward measurable economic outcomes, including tokens per watt, tokens per dollar, and time to revenue. See how ecosystem collaboration is unlocking new opportunities for enterprises and service providers to profitably scale AI revenue.

### AI Cluster Benchmarks - Establishing a standardized evaluation system for Superpods
Speakers: Zhenzhong Bao (Huawei), Yun Zhao (Huawei)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9393/ | materials: https://github.com/AISBench/benchmark
This presentation focuses on two benchmark projects: ClusterBench and AISBench. ClusterBench reflects the core characteristics of general-purpose computing superpods. It covers more than 10 workload scenarios, establishes a 3-layer metric system, and supports four stress testing modes. It emphasizes the validation of scenarios such as low-latency RPC, ultra-large virtual machines, live migration, and cloud big data storage, with metrics like workload throughput, VM density, migration time, remote memory access latency, bandwidth, etc. AISBench selects more than four scenarios of AI test workloads, covering mainstream model tracks such as MOE models, multimodal models, and AI Agents, and established a 3-layer evaluation system comprising reliability, inference, and integrated training-inference metrics. It has the powerful processing capability to support 150k high concurrency and 250k high requests, which reflects the hardware-software collaboration of intelligent computing superpods.

### From Benchmark to Bottleneck: Agentic AI for Datacenter Workload Analysis
Speakers: Meha Virapandiane (Intel), Ho-Sup Chung (Intel)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9472/
Benchmarking datacenter platforms requires two labor-intensive phases: running benchmarks correctly across diverse hardware, then diagnosing what is limiting performance. Neither phase scales to the speed of modern silicon bring-up or fleet qualification. This session presents an open, end-to-end agentic AI framework on GitHub Copilot that automates both phases, applied to Meta's DCPerf open benchmark suite of workloads. A 10-skill Copilot agent installs, runs, monitors, and reports on any DCPerf workload from a single natural language prompt — results in under 3 hours with zero manual steps. Measured on a 2-socket Intel Xeon server, performance exceeded the DCPerf community baseline by ~2x across workloads. A second agentic layer automatically identifies hardware bottlenecks using Top-Down Microarchitecture Analysis. The data is collected using standard Linux perf stat on Intel Xeon. No proprietary tools required — anyone with a Linux server and AI copilot can reproduce every result.

### Model-Based Testing for Open-Source Software Components: Public Modeling Library and Interaction-Level Validation
Speakers: Mengqi Liu (Alibaba), Ali Kheradmand (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9357/
Model-based testing (MBT) generates tests by systematically traversing developer-maintained behavior models. Unlike manual or LLM-assisted approaches, it derives tests with strong coverage guarantees. This talk presents practical MBT adoption in open source development. We present an open-source Python modeling library that allows developers to write executable behavior models and generate tests via symbolic execution. Released with related infrastructure code, it provides a foundation for adopting MBT in real-world development. Next, we extend MBT beyond single-API unit tests to interaction-level validation. We use a case study to demonstrate how to model interacting components, enumerate diverse API call sequences, and generate tests verifying state consistency along these sequences. It finds bugs that isolated unit tests cannot. Finally, we explore using LLMs to draft models from RFCs and design documents, showing how formal methods and generative AI complement each other.

### AI Computing Continuum: Building Open Infrastructure Standards for the Next Wave of AI
Speakers: Andi Huels (https://bluskyaidatacenters.com/), Jacob Yundt (CoreWeave), Sanee Iqbal (RealTime Labs), Kevin Tubbs (VDURA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9364/
As AI inference expands beyond hyperscale campuses into regional data centers, enterprise environments, telecom facilities, sovereign AI deployments, industrial sites, and NeoClouds, the industry faces a new challenge: building an open AI Computing Continuum. Today's infra remains fragmented across power, cooling, networking, accelerators, operations, and workload portability. This session explores the emerging standards gaps preventing AI from scaling efficiently across diverse deployment models, particularly in facilities not originally designed for AI. Topics include open architectures, interoperability, distributed networking, sustainability, orchestration, observability, and workload mobility. The discussion will examine how OCP can help define reference architectures, collaborative frameworks, and open standards that connect AI infra across centralized and distributed environments, accelerating the next phase of AI growth from the hyperscale core to every shade of locality.

### Breaking up the Monolith - An Open Architecture for Multi-rack Scale-up Domain of 5000+ GPUs
Speakers: Promode Nedungadi (Eridu), Dan Daly (Delos Data)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9809/
As models pass trillion parameters and adopt mixture-of-experts and disaggregated prefill/decode, the binding constraint is no longer FLOPS, but how many accelerators talk at memory-semantic speed in a single hop. Most scale-up domains stop at 8 to 72 GPUs, forcing parallelism onto a slower scale-out network where every hop adds latency and tail variance. This session presents a flat, one-hop scale-up domain connecting 5k GPUs as a single fabric with every GPU reaching every other in one hop and shows why that shrinks collective-communication time, tightens token-generation tail latency, and lets a frontier model fit entirely inside a single domain. We detail a practical implementation: a GPU server bringing all scale-up I/O to the front panel, making a domain this size deployable through cabling and high radix switch, not a fixed backplane. We close on how open, OCP-aligned optics and standards can deliver this performance at scale.

### Tahan: An Open, Ethernet-Based Scale-Up Switch for Meta's MTIA Accelerators
Speakers: Ashutosh Grewal (Meta), Vimal Vasudevan (Meta Platforms inc.)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9723/
Scale-up accelerator interconnects have historically been proprietary and vendor-locked. We present Tahan, Meta's first scale-up switch, and the open Ethernet-based scale-up fabric it enables for MTIA 300 accelerators. Built on merchant 25T/51.2 Tbps switch silicon running FBOSS, Tahan reuses the same lossless RoCE transport (routed IPv6, PFC, AI-tuned QoS) as our scale-out network, unifying both fabrics under one NOS and eliminating proprietary software and operational silos. The fabric delivers 6.4 Tbps per accelerator over 8× 800GbE passive copper links in a liquid-cooled rack interconnecting 16 accelerators via 4 switches in a non-blocking topology. We cover Tahan's hardware architecture, in-rack copper and liquid-cooling design, and accelerator-to-accelerator latency and bandwidth. We also outline a next-gen fabric: 8 switches on 102.4 Tbps silicon interconnecting 72 accelerators per rack as we adopt OCP ESUN as the scale-up dataplane.

### Validation methodologies for ESUN ecosystem
Speakers: Venkat Pullela (Keysight), Manoj Wadekar (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9852/
The OCP ESUN specification defines a standardized Ethernet architecture for scale-up AI fabrics, integrating lossless transport mechanisms—PFC, CBFC, and LLR—with a compact ESUN Header for improved bandwidth efficiency. As ESUN implementations emerge, the absence of a formal conformance framework presents a key barrier to deployment readiness. Validating these capabilities at 800GE and 1.6T speeds demands realistic AI workload emulation, precise tail-latency measurement, and robust multi-vendor interoperability testing. This paper presents an evaluation of an ESUN-enabled switch and examines the critical validation challenges of ESUN-based scale-up networks. It explores benchmarking approaches for objectively comparing ESUN implementations across vendors—accelerating ecosystem maturity, deployment confidence, and broad industry adoption.

### Test Methodology and Practice for Multi-Accelerator Copper Scaleup Domain Interconnect and Communication Library
Speakers: Yao Rong (Meta), Karthik Ganesan (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9709/
AI training clusters are moving to rack‑scale multi‑accelerator domains, making copper interconnect validation a first‑order design concern. This session presents a practical test methodology for using MI450X‑class GPU systems as an example using high‑bandwidth copper fabrics to form large accelerator scale‑up domains. We cover link‑level margining, topology discovery, congestion and latency characterization, collective and RDMA‑style traffic patterns, and rack‑scale fault injection. A vendor‑neutral communication library abstraction encodes topology and link health, enabling reproducible performance characterization and faster bring‑up across accelerator SKUs on open, OCP‑aligned platforms.

### Enabling AI Networking @ Scale with Multi-path Reliable Connections (MRC)
Speakers: Guohan Lu (OpenAI), Eric Spada (Broadcom Inc.)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8964/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/documents/ocp-mrc-1-0-pdf
As AI clusters scale toward hundreds of thousands of XPUs, network resiliency, congestion management, and tail-latency control are becoming critical challenges for distributed AI training and inference. This session introduces Multi-Path Reliable Connection (MRC), now an Open Compute Project (OCP) specification designed to enable resilient, high-performance Ethernet fabrics for large-scale AI infrastructure. MRC combines intelligent multi-path transport, adaptive load balancing, reliable delivery, and path- aware congestion management to maximize utilization while maintaining low latency across massive AI clusters. The session will highlight how MRC enables simplified Ethernet architectures that scale efficiently while improving reliability, operational efficiency, and power efficiency, while accelerating open, Ethernet-based AI infrastructure through ecosystem collaboration and OCP innovation.

### Network Configurations for Optimal AI Fabrics including MRC and SRv6
Speakers: Mohammad Hanif (Broadcom), Arihant Jain (Arista Networks)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9774/ | materials: https://blogs.arista.com/blog/three-genius-ideas-for-ai-fabrics
As AI clusters scale, network inefficiencies such as oversubscription and link failures increasingly impact Job Completion Time (JCT). This session presents an open, standards-based approach to building scalable Ethernet AI fabrics. We examine multi-planar leaf-spine architectures that improve scale, resiliency, and hardware efficiency through parallel network planes. We then introduce Multipath Reliable Connection (MRC), an open OCP specification that decouples reliable transport from physical paths using Entropy Values (EVs), packet spraying, out-of-order delivery, and active congestion mitigation. Finally, we demonstrate how MRC combined with Segment Routing over IPv6 (SRv6) enables deterministic load balancing, explicit path control, real-time path monitoring, and fast failover beyond the limits of traditional ECMP.

### UEC vs MRC: A Comparative Analysis of Next-Generation Networking Standards for Large-Scale DC Deployments
Speakers: S. Kamran Naqvi (Broadcom), Mohammad Hanif (Broadcom)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9499/
The rapid growth of data center infrastructure has exposed fundamental scalability limitations in traditional RDMA-based networking. Two emerging standards, the Ultra Ethernet Consortium (UEC) and Multipath Reliable Connection (MRC), address these challenges through distinct but partially overlapping approaches. Both adopt packet spraying for load balancing and source-based congestion control by default. However, they diverge significantly in scope and philosophy. MRC extends existing RoCEv2 concepts, offering a familiar evolutionary path, yet omits receiver-based congestion control and provides no guidance at the network or link layer. UEC, by contrast, is a clean-slate design delivering a comprehensive stack spanning transport through the link layer. This session compares UEC and MRC across key dimensions like load balancing, congestion management, deployment model, and architectural completeness to evaluate their suitability for next-generation data center deployments at scale.

### Demystifying MRC and UET: A Panel of Experts
Speakers: Eric Spada (Broadcom Inc.), Rip Sohan (Broadcom), Keith Underwood (HPE), Mark Handley (OpenAI)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9428/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/documents/ocp-mrc-1-0-pdf | pdf: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04333
Ultra Ethernet Transport (UET) is a next‑generation RDMA transport designed to enable high‑performance AI/ML and HPC applications over multipath best‑effort Ethernet networks. Multiple Reliable Connection (MRC), on the other hand, extends the RoCE Reliable Connection Transport, integrating UET concepts to add multipath, resiliency and fast-failover for large-scale multiplane networks. In this panel, protocol architects for both the UET and MRC transports will provide insight into why each was created, their commonalities, differences and how they serve AI and HPC workloads. They will further outline how UET extends MRC to provide additional functionality, efficiency, scalability and performance. There will be ample time for audience interaction, so bring your toughest questions for the UET and MRC experts.

### Powering AI at Scale: Inside the UALink Ecosystem
Speakers: Priyank Shukla (Synopsys), Helia Naeimi (Astera Labs), Matt Holdrege (Microchip), Josh Collier (Intel)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9202/ | materials: https://ualinkconsortium.org/specification/
As AI workloads continue to scale in complexity and size in 2026, cloud service providers, system OEMs, and silicon and IP vendors are seeking more efficient and scalable approaches to address scale-up AI challenges. The UALink Consortium defines a low-latency, high-bandwidth interconnect purpose-built for communication between accelerators and switches within AI compute pods, enabling next-generation disaggregated architectures. In this panel, UALink experts will explore how the evolving UALink ecosystem is supporting improved performance, power efficiency, and cost optimization while promoting interoperability and supply chain diversity. The discussion will also highlight emerging applications and real-world use cases across AI/ML infrastructure, offering attendees insights into deployment considerations and the future direction of high-performance accelerator connectivity.

### SUE-T - An Ethernet transport for Scale-Up Networking
Speakers: Tom Emmons (Arista Networks), Karen Schramm (Broadcom)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8935/
As machine learning and AI inference workloads expand in complexity, the need for parallel processing power increases significantly. Scaling up an XPU cluster to a rack or multi-rack level is needed to enhance its ability to process larger datasets, train deeper neural networks, handle more simultaneous tasks, reduce execution time, and improve overall system efficiency. The Scale-up Ethernet Transport (SUE-T) project defines a framework to provide low-latency, high-bandwidth connectivity for XPU scale-up networks based on Ethernet. A SUE-T instance moves memory transactions between XPUs. This transport solution is suitable for deploying over Ethernet networks, including those defined by ESUN. This talk will delve into the SUE-T specification, explaining how it meets the challenging demands of scale-up networking and the practical benefits to next-generation XPU designs.

### Fast switching OCS for AI and Hyperscale Data Centers: Benefit, Technologies and Potential Applications.
Speakers: James Burch (Lumotive), Vivek Sarathy (iPronics)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9589/
Optical Circuit Switching (OCS) is a strong foundation for dynamically reconfigurable, high-performance network fabrics that support growing AI/ML and GPU workloads. While not every OCS use case requires fast switching, its value increases as system-level reconfiguration becomes faster for applications such as dynamic GPU interconnects, workload-aware topology adaptation, and rapidly changing traffic patterns. In this context, fast switching is defined not only by device-level speed, but by end-to-end reconfiguration time, including control-plane coordination and transceiver lock behavior. This presentation reviews key fast-switching approaches, including MEMS, liquid crystal, silicon photonics, metasurfaces, and hybrid technologies, their system bottlenecks, and highlights emerging use cases such as dynamic GPU fabrics, topology-aware optimization, MoE traffic acceleration, and disaggregated inference fabrics.

### Co-Designing AI Fabrics: Inside Google’s Virgo Network and the Campus-as-a-Computer
Speakers: Sunil Ahluwalia (Google), JK Lee (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9543/
The explosive growth of Generative AI and foundational models demands a fundamental rethink of physical cloud architecture. To support massive training runs and real-time Agentic AI inference, traditional data centers must now operate as single, unified, campus-scale supercomputers. In this session, we detail the design of Virgo, a megascale AI data center fabric powered by the open-source SONiC NOS that underpins the AI Hypercomputer. By building upon open standards and hardware ecosystems, Virgo incorporates technologies like open-source RDMA transport (Falcon), Congestion Signaling (CSIG) and High-Speed Telemetry (HST) to seamlessly handle synchronized traffic bursts and deliver on its core value proposition of predictable low latency at massive scale. Coupled with this fine-grained telemetry, the multi-plane Virgo architecture maximizes "goodput" and resilience for the world's most demanding ML workloads.

### Multi-planar AI Scale Out Fabrics
Speakers: Devang Patel (AMD), Alan Judge (Arista Networks)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8991/ | materials: https://blogs.arista.com/blog/three-genius-ideas-for-ai-fabrics
Building highly performant and resilient AI Clusters at large scale is hard. Can we make it easier and remain open? Work continues to enable the next generation of Scale Out networks based on Ethernet and open standards. These networks typically leverage multi-plane architectures and we will explain the concept, discuss the challenges, and present potential solutions. This talk showcases collaboration between AMD and Arista Networks to leverage OCP specifications and build an open solution. Topics: System configuration and the benefits of the OCP ORW form factor Scale Out network design and how multi-planar designs reduce cost and enhance reliability Challenges facing such deployments, such failure detection, load balancing, and cabling complexity The role of the AI NIC and the OCP Multipath Reliable Connection (MRC) specification in delivering an open solution Combining everything into a working system

### End-to-End AI backend fabric design
Speakers: Sandeep Balani (Broadcom), Mehak Mahajan (Broadcom)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8933/
This session talks about the AI network fabric design's. The session focuses on both end-to-end fabric design which includes NIC, Switches, Optics, Cables etc. The main focus would be Advanced RoCE capabilities defined as part of Ultra-Ethernet Consortium like Packet Spray ( NIC packet spray (UDP source port, Switch packet spray (DLB, AR) ), Out-Of-Order Data Placement, Selective retransmission / ack, advanced congestion control and telemetry capabilities. The session will also focus on various topologies with switch scheduled fabric and end-point scheduled fabric and will also provide examples of how large AI hyperscalers, Neo-Clouds, and CSP's are deploying these capabilities in their AI fabric today.

### From Single Data Centers to Multi-Region AI Clusters: The Rise of Scale Across
Speakers: Spencer Giacalone (Ciena), Helen Xenos (Ciena)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8913/
AI training environments are evolving into distributed infrastructures spanning multiple campuses and regions. As we move toward million-plus GPU deployments, a new architectural paradigm is emerging: Scale Across. This session explores how Scale Across complements existing Scale Up and Scale Out architectures, and why it is becoming essential for next-generation AI training. Attendees will gain an understanding of the size and growth of modern AI clusters, the networking challenges associated with distributed training, and the role of packet and optical networking in enabling synchronized AI workloads across distance. We will discuss architectural considerations, including congestion management, latency, jitter, and optical transport requirements, as well as the technologies being deployed and developed to support Scale Across. This session is intended for architects, engineers, and planners seeking to understand how AI networking is evolving beyond the data center.

### Scale Across: To 'Go Forward" or To "Go Backwards", That is the question
Speakers: Jai Kumar (Broadcom), Tom Emmons (Arista Networks)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9157/
As large language models continue to grow in size and training becomes increasingly distributed, AI infrastructure is expanding beyond traditional scale-up and scale-out boundaries. The resulting multi-site and geographically distributed scale-across fabrics introduce challenges that differ significantly from those encountered in conventional data center networks. Increased propagation delays, long-distance links, and bandwidth-constrained interconnects require a rethinking of how network telemetry is collected, transported, and consumed by the congestion control loops at the end points or used for multi-pathing. A central question is how fabric state should be communicated: should telemetry signals move forward with traffic, propagate backward toward sources, or leverage a hybrid approach?

### Scale-across Networking: Panel discussion on routing and transport equipment implications
Speakers: Jeff Rahn (Meta), Yawei Yin (Microsoft), Xiguang - Henry Wu (Broadcom), Tad Hofmeister (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9622/ | materials: https://blogs.microsoft.com/blog/2025/11/12/infinite-scale-the-architecture-behind-the-azure-ai-superfactory/
As AI cluster size grows significantly, physical power and space constraints limit traditional "scale-out" AI backend networks. To break these boundaries, the industry is adopting "Scale-Across" networking that extends AI fabrics across longer distances to scale clusters beyond 100k to 0.5M to 1M XPUs. However, scale-across introduces unique technical challenges including propagation delays, higher oversubscription, and massive synchronized bursty traffic that diverge sharply from conventional cloud DCI. This panel brings together industry experts to demystify the scale-across use-case, exploring its real-world rollout and discussing architectures and designs. Panelists will analyze key network requirements, focusing on coherent pluggables (ZR/ZR+), deep packet buffering to absorb regional latency, wire-rate security, and advanced congestion control to mitigate fiber scarcity. Panel speakers from Meta, Microsoft, Google, Broadcom and Arista

### MX v1.1: Advancing Open Standards for Low-Precision AI with MicroXcaling formats
Speakers: Partha Maji (Microsoft)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9580/
The OCP MicroXcaling (MX) specification defines an open, interoperable framework for low-precision AI data formats using shared block scaling to improve performance, memory efficiency, and energy consumption. This talk presents the key updates in MX v1.1 specification, including refined conversion semantics from scalar vectors to MX formats, improved rounding and quantization behavior across FP8/FP6/FP4, and standardization of the UE8M0 scale type. We also discuss clarified handling of edge cases such as NaNs and out-of-range values, which improves consistency across hardware and software implementations. These updates strengthen MX as a practical foundation for sub-8-bit AI numerics, enabling scalable deployment across diverse architectures while maintaining interoperability within the broader OCP ecosystem.

### Node-to-Rack Efficiency for Real-World AI Inference: Arm and FuriosaAI for Scalable Open Infrastructure
Speakers: Jihoon Yoon (Furiosa AI), Satadal Bhattacharjee (Arm)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9377/
AI inference moves from isolated accelerator performance to production-scale deployment, efficiency needs to be evaluated across the full system: host CPU, accelerator, software stack, server node, and rack-level architecture. In this session, Arm and FuriosaAI will explore how Arm-based host infrastructure and FuriosaAI RNGD accelerators can work together to improve real-world inference efficiency from node to rack. Building from node-level architecture, the session will then discuss how these efficiency gains translate into rack-level considerations, including performance density, power and thermal envelope, serviceability, and deployability in OCP-aligned infrastructure environments.

### Scaling Purpose-Built AI Models via OCP Aligned Distributed Inference Fabrics
Speakers: Art Fewell (Hedgehog), Arun Solleti (Celestica)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9952/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/community/cluster-designs-for-ai
This presentation will outline a practical approach to scale massive, purpose-built AI models across multi-node cluster domains when computational footprints exceed a single server chassis. We will demonstrate how to adapt the design guidelines from the Open Cluster Designs Aligned AI Inference Fabric Reference Architecture to deploy a standardized Distributed Inference Profile. During the session, we will showcase a scale-out fabric strategy using the Open Network Fabric control plane and SONiC to coordinate workloads across an interconnected, multi-node scale-up network. We will introduce a prescriptive data-plane tuning model that leverages RoCE features to safeguard cross-host tensor-parallel communication paths without manual engineering. Finally, we will illustrate how enforcing consistent ECMP hashing seeds across leaf-spine layers minimizes tail-latency variance , allowing organizations to secure the deterministic response times required for production serving.

### BUILDING AI RACK SYSTEM WITH OPEN STANDARDS
Speakers: Shangxin Du (AMD), Mike Wong (HPE)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9844/ | materials: https://www.amd.com/en/blogs/2025/amd-helios-ai-rack-built-on-metas-2025-ocp-design.html
Interconnect more GPU’s at rack scale with scale-up fabrics has proven to be the most effective way in creating a larger AI system without sacrificing the desired low latency that is critical for both HPC and AI workloads. The collaborations amongst AMD, Broadcom, and HPE will deliver the industry’s first fully integrated and validated Scale-up AI Rack Systems based on open standards, through AMD’s Helios AI Rack reference architecture Starting with the foundation of OCP’s Open Rack Wide (ORW) standard, and combined with innovations including AMD’s Ryzen V3000 CPU, MI455 GPU’s and UALink, Broadcom’s Tomahawk 6 ASIC, as well as scale-up fabric designed and developed by HPE Networking operating with SONiC, this rack-scale AI system delivers unprecedented performance, yet with none of the proprietary standards or protocols.

### USB to Rule Them All: One Manageability Interface for AI Platforms
Speakers: MARIUSZ ORIOL (NVIDIA), Bharat Pillili (Microsoft), Samer El-Haj-Mahmoud (Arm), Mohan Kumar (Oracle Cloud Infrastructure), Supreeth Venkatesh (AMD)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9581/ | materials: https://drive.google.com/file/d/18x57cqvXWFEJAKuVJaa0ClAzHxiK-tN5/view?usp=drivesdk
As AI racks become denser and more complex, manageability is becoming a critical factor in scalability challenge. In this panel, NVIDIA will be joined by AMD, Arm, Microsoft and OCI to discuss how the industry can align on open, interoperable management models for next-generation AI infrastructure. Panelists will explore reducing proprietary sideband fragmentation, accelerating OCP specification adoption, and converging around approaches such as USB-based manageability, OCP Recovery over USB, Caliptra workflows, OBMF, MCTP over USB, and emerging NC-SI use cases. The discussion will focus on what vendors must do together to make AI racks more reliable, manageable, and scalable.

### Unified Telemetry for Next-Generation Rack-Scale Infrastructure
Speakers: Antonis Michaloliakos (Meta), Nischay Tata (AMD)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9555/
Next-gen AI platforms chase ultra-low latency by moving switching into silicon -- but removing external PCIe switches (e.g. AMD Helios: Venice CPUs, MI455X GPUs) erases key instrumentation, leaving engineers blind to host-side congestion. Worse, under load naive polling can itself cause much of the congestion it measures (the "Observer Effect"). AMD and Meta are co-designing a unified telemetry architecture across CPU, GPU, AI-NIC, and OOB management. Guardrails -- affinity and tiered Production/Debug verbosity -- expose boundary telemetry safely, while combined In-Band/OOB keeps data available without contention. The metrics power three pillars: deep debugging, intelligent scheduling around degraded nodes, and fleet health at thousand-GPU scale. Using Helios as a proving ground, we lay out a path to standardize open telemetry interfaces -- a vendor-neutral boundary-telemetry schema and verbosity contract -- for next-generation AI infrastructure.

### Unified Full-Stack Telemetry for AI Systems: From Silicon to Cluster: Unifying In-Band, Out-of-Band, and Bulk Telemetry for AI Systems
Speakers: Janusz Jurski (Intel), Karthik Raman (Intel)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9023/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/documents/ocp-open-systems-for-ai-whitepaper-v1-0-0-final.pdf
AI clusters expose telemetry across multiple disconnected layers — host software, platform controllers, accelerators, rack management, and orchestration — but OCP does not yet define a unified system-level telemetry model across these layers. This proposal introduces a unified full-stack telemetry discussion for the OCP AI Clusters workgroup, focused on AI systems rather than isolated components. The goal is to explore how in-band, out-of-band, and bulk telemetry can be composed across CPU, GPU, accelerator, platform, rack, and orchestration layers using common identity, timing, and ownership boundaries. The intent is to align existing standards and open interfaces into a reusable model that can improve bottleneck attribution, root-cause analysis, and orchestration-aware observability for future AI systems.

### Achieving AI Cluster Reliability at Scale: Lessons in Reducing Workload Interruptions
Speakers: Bob Wang (Meta), Ramachandra Reddy Kallam (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8849/
As AI clusters scale from 32K to 100K+ GPUs, workload interruptions become the dominant barrier to training efficiency. At scale, even modest per-host failure rates compound into frequent job disruptions—our GBx00 clusters initially experienced significantly higher interruption rates than mature deployments. This talk presents a framework for diagnosing and reducing AI workload interruptions. We introduce a root cause classification methodology using Perfetto time trees that correlates host telemetry, firmware logs, network events, and workload signals to attribute each interruption to its underlying failure mode. We cover key contributors—NVLink failures, HBM fragmentation, thermal failures, SDC, and firmware bugs—and how data-driven root cause analysis fed into next-gen hardware requirements, influencing vendor decisions on error reporting, fault isolation, and resilience. We discuss how these learnings and diagnostic frameworks can benefit the broader OCP community.

### Customer-Contextual Fault Management for Bare Metal GPU Infrastructure
Speakers: Aimer Bhat (Oracle), Jim Britton (Oracle)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8775/ | materials: https://docs.oracle.com/en-us/iaas/Content/Compute/Tasks/non-terminating-repair.htm
Large-scale GPU infrastructure introduces new reliability and serviceability challenges, especially in bare metal and raw metal environments where platform telemetry access may be incomplete or insufficient to determine customer impact. In complex GPU systems connected through scale-up fabrics such as NVLink and scale-out fabrics such as InfiniBand or RoCE, a single component fault can trigger cascading events across the system and application layers. At the same time, built-in redundancy may generate error signals that are not workload impacting. This session shares engineering learnings from developing a customer-collaborative fault management architecture for GPU fleet operations. The approach uses customer-observed workload impact, such as application failures or degraded job execution, as an additional signal alongside hardware telemetry. We will discuss how this context can improve triage, reduce false positives, prioritize repairs, and inform emerging OCP work-streams.

### Scaling AI Inference with Arm AGI CPU, Rebellions RebelCard, and OCP ORv3 Rack Design
Speakers: Jennifer Glore (Rebellions), Dilip Ramachandran (Arm)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9397/
As AI inference scales, efficiency is becoming the key constraint for deployment and monetization across data centers. In this session, Arm and Rebellions will show how Arm AGI CPU-based systems integrate with Rebellions’ RebelCard accelerators in OCP ORv3-aligned rack designs, including emerging 5U server configurations. The discussion will focus on how CPU-accelerator integration, memory bandwidth, and workload characteristics affect energy efficiency and deployment cost at scale. Particular attention will be given to the rise of agentic AI workloads, which introduce fundamentally different execution patterns compared to traditional user-prompted inference. Rather than a single prompt-response interaction, agentic systems generate continuous orchestration activity, tool usage, retrieval operations, reasoning loops, and inter-agent communication, increasing the importance of efficient CPU-accelerator coordination and balanced system design.

### Cluster-less Validation of AI Accelerator Silicon Under Real Workloads
Speakers: Matt Bergeron (Meta), Ashutosh Kumar (Keysight Technologies), Cosmin-Nicolae Prunaru (Keysight Technologies)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9602/ | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-PRs1eVF3nY
Validating a new AI accelerator chip against realistic distributed-training behavior traditionally demands a large physical cluster — a costly, late-stage step that stretches time-to-production. We show how to validate a real accelerator chip without a real cluster. Modern silicon integrates multiple compute units and high-speed NICs into a scale-up fabric, which real workloads stress — through tensor- and expert-parallel collectives — in ways synthetic benchmarks cannot. In our methodology the system-under-test, with all compute units and NICs engaged, perceives itself as part of a large emulated cluster while replaying communication traces captured from real training runs. Analyzing per-NIC and per-rank breakdowns, collective bandwidth, completion times, and congestion telemetry localizes bottlenecks and defects on the chip itself, improving coverage and reducing time-to-production.

### Next Generation Optically Integrated Platform Solution for AI Data Centers
Speakers: John Chiang (Celestica)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9617/
The demand for higher performance, density and scalable AI clusters is becoming exponentially prevalent. This is creating the need for more optically integrated solutions to address next generation inter-rack-dense and intra-rack-scale architectures in support for the new agentic workloads in AI Data Centers. Celestica will provide an overview for a next generation open and optically integrated AI-fabric solution based on Co-Packaged Optics (CPO) technology used for scale-up and scale-out use-cases. This solution leverages innovations in SONiC considering specifications from the Ultra Ethernet Consortium (UEC) and OCP Ethernet Scale-Up Network (ESUN) standards with advanced optics telemetry.

### Secure Multi-Tenancy for Open AI Clusters
Speakers: Manish Vachharajani (Hedgehog), Art Fewell (Hedgehog)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9980/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/community/cluster-designs-for-ai
Enterprises, neoclouds, and service providers increasingly need shared GPU infrastructure with cloud-like tenant isolation, rapid reprovisioning, and self-service networking. AI clusters make this more challenging than traditional cloud environments: GPU performance requirements limit host-based isolation, tenants may require different kernels, drivers, or Kubernetes configurations, and RoCE fabrics must maintain full performance. This session explores the multi-tenancy model in the Open Cluster Designs AI Training and Inference Fabric Reference Architectures. We will show how tenant isolation can be enforced in the physical network using VPC-style abstractions built on VRFs, EVPN/VXLAN, route policy, and controlled external connectivity. Attendees will learn how open Ethernet AI fabrics can deliver isolation, policy enforcement, gateway services, and operational consistency while maintaining performance and supporting auditable tenant lifecycle operations.

### AI‑Ready Network Infrastructure: The Practical Realities of High‑Power, Liquid‑Cooled Rack Integration
Speakers: Rob Nance (Jabil), Arihant Jain (Arista Networks)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9942/
AI‑ready networks are pushing integrated rack deployments to new limits. As fabrics scale to thousands of nodes, networking racks must support liquid cooling, rising rack‑level power, dense optical connectivity, and delivery rates of hundreds of racks per week. These demands expose challenges in supply chain orchestration, factory integration, thermal and power envelope alignment, and ensuring interoperability across switches, optics, and cooling components. This session examines the practical realities of building and deploying integrated networking racks for AI systems at hyperscale. We highlight lessons learned, emerging requirements for liquid‑cooled and high‑power network gear, and the platform‑level standards needed to accelerate safe, repeatable, high‑velocity deployment.

### Evolution of Congestion Control in AI Ethernet Fabrics
Speakers: Mohammad Hanif (Broadcom), Mehak Mahajan (Broadcom)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9761/
As AI clusters scale to hundreds of thousands of GPUs, network fabric behavior increasingly determines overall system efficiency. Large-scale training workloads generate synchronized communication patterns, severe incast, and sensitivity to tail latency that challenge traditional congestion control mechanisms in lossless Ethernet networks. Achieving efficient Job Completion Time (JCT) requires coordinated congestion management across both NICs and switches, with each contributing complementary signals and control actions. This session examines the evolution of congestion control for AI back-end networks. We will review the operating principles, limitations, and deployment considerations of existing approaches and compare them with emerging mechanisms designed for modern AI traffic patterns. The session will provide a framework for understanding the trade-offs between these approaches and the role they play in the design of next-generation AI Ethernet fabrics.

### From Training to Tokens: Revolutionizing AI Inference with In-Network Compute
Speakers: Caleb Shetland (Astera Labs), Helia Naeimi (Astera Labs)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9786/
The AI landscape is moving from training models to optimizing inference at scale. As LLMs evolve toward MoE architectures, inference isn’t just compute-bound but communication-bound. High Time-to-First-Token and erratic Output-Tokens-Per-Time result from "AllReduce stall" where accelerators wait on a passive fabric. This session explores how In-Network Compute (INC) and Hypercast™ transform the fabric into an active participant, offloading collective operations like AllReduce to reduce latency by up to 50%. We showcase this vision in the PCIe Fabric with a real-world example and how an INC foundation enables a multivendor, memory-semantic scale up fabric that treats the entire rack as a single, low-latency compute unit. We also cover scale-up protocols such as UALink 2.0 , with INC in the spec, ensuring continuation for memory-semantic, low latency scale-up fabric.

### Beyond GPUs: Building the Open Operations Layer for AI Factories and Token Economies
Speakers: Naren Shankar (hosted.ai), Peter Sheh (Crusoe), Jonmichael Hands (FarmGPU), Preeti Vasaikar (PerceptEye)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9413/
As AI infrastructure evolves from GPU centric clusters to heterogeneous AI factories, operators face a challenge: how to consistently monitor, orchestrate, and optimize increasingly diverse infrastructure spanning GPUs, inference ASICs, networking accelerators, storage systems, power infrastructure, and emerging domain specific processors across hyperscale facilities, NeoClouds, sovereign AI deployments, enterprises, and telecom networks. Telemetry, health reporting, diagnostics, management interfaces, automation, and lifecycle operations often vary across vendors and infrastructure domains, creating barriers. Drawing on work across ClusterMax, Redfish profiles, Open Network Fabrics, hardware management standards, and NeoCloud operational requirements, panelists will discuss how open telemetry, interoperable control planes, standardized management interfaces, and autonomous operations can enable infrastructure portability across vendors, accelerators, and deployment environments.

## AI Computing Continuum

### AI Computing Continuum: Extending the Open Data Center Ecosystem Across Cloud, Edge, and Industry
Speakers: Masahisa Kawashima (NTT Corporation), Dirk Blevins (Intel), Vincent Nguyen (Hewlett Packard Enterprise)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8989/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/community/aicc
As AI adoption accelerates across industries, connectivity between industrial environments and AI cloud infrastructure is becoming critical. To address this, the Open Compute Project (OCP) and the IOWN Global Forum have launched the AI Computing Continuum (AICC), a joint initiative to unify distributed resources across cloud, edge, telecommunications, and industrial domains through high-bandwidth, low-latency networks. This keynote will present a shared vision for the AI Computing Continuum and explore how AI workloads are expanding beyond traditional hyperscale environments and why a unified computing space across cloud and industry domains is becoming essential. It will also introduce representative use cases and highlight how open, community-driven collaboration between OCP and the IOWN Global Forum accelerates the development of interoperable platforms, networking technologies, and infrastructure specifications.

### Breaking the Data Center Walls: Unifying AI Fabrics for Agentic AI at the Edge
Speakers: Prakash Jain (Celestica)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9769/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/products?cloud_products%5Bquery%5D=celestica
This session explores the transition of AI infrastructure from centralized hyperscaler silos to a unified, distributed " Scale-Across " model. While existing OCP frameworks manage local "Scale-Up" and "Scale-Out" environments effectively, the emergence of Agentic AI, requiring autonomous, low-latency edge decision-making, demands an integrated across the domain strategy. We propose a ‘ Distributed Rack & Network Fabric Architecture’ that combines an ‘ End-to-End Control Plane’ with ‘ Agentic AI–Driven Management’. By separating endpoints from routing locators, this approach enables ‘ Inter-Domain Coordination’, facilitating high-performance workloads across various geographic locations and heterogeneous hardware. Architecture features include: Global substrate to extend backend fabrics over WAN/RAN. Support for 400 & 800 Gbps bandwidth and sub-microsecond latency to facilitate memory-semantic communication and RDMA. Integration of open community work across OCP, SONiC, UEC & IETF

### AI Computing Continuum Use Cases and Collaboration Items
Speakers: Naoto Umemori (NTT DATA), Hidetsugu Sugiyama (Red Hat), Xiaojing Zhang (NTT DOCOMO BUSINESS, Inc.), Junichi Sugiyama (1Finity), Koji Watanabe (NTT)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/10121/
Lightning Talks Featuring: Reach-Across for Sovereign AI (NTT DOCOMO Business) Sync-Across for Financial Service Platform (NTT DATA) Scale-Across for Distributed LLM Inference over IOWN APN (Red Hat) Reach-Across with APN-ready Smart NIC (1Finity) Resource Pooling with Composable Hardware (NTT)

### Two Years of x86 Ecosystem Collaboration: Open Infrastructure, AI Readiness, and Developer Predictability
Speakers: Jeff McVeigh (Intel), Stephen Watt (Red Hat), Andrew Wheeler (HPE), Martin Dixon (Google), Jon Lange (Microsoft)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8905/ | materials: https://x86ecosystem.org/
As AI, security, virtualization, & cloud-scale infrastructure demands accelerate, the industry needs open, predictable, & interoperable compute platforms. In the spirit of OCP’s open collaboration model, the x86 EAG brings AMD, Intel, cloud providers, OEMs, OS & SW leaders, & open-source voices together to reduce fragmentation and improve platform readiness across the x86 ecosystem. Marking two years of collaboration, this panel will share lessons from cross-vendor architectural alignment and its impact on software portability, OS & runtime readiness, virtualization, confidential computing, firmware, telemetry, & infrastructure deployment. The discussion will connect these outcomes to OCP priorities & projects including Server, Open Platform Firmware, Hardware Management, Security, and Open Systems for AI. Attendees will learn how open ecosystem collaboration can improve predictability, simplify deployment, & enable scalable AI-era infrastructure from edge to hyperscale data centers.

### AI-Native Edge MHS 1.0
Speakers: David Heinrich (HPE), Dirk Blevins (Intel)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9056/
The AI Native Edge-MHS specification provides modular Host Processor Module (HPM) definitions capable of enabling optimized modular hardware solutions to standardize mechanical, electrical, and thermal interfaces that allow interoperable multi node AI systems to be implemented in the targeted environments supporting CPUs, GPUs, and Accelerators for AI Inferencing outside of the Hyperscale Data Center.

### The Open Adapter Card - Light
Speakers: Dirk Blevins (Intel), Vincent Nguyen (Hewlett Packard Enterprise)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/10119/
The Open Adapter Card - Light

### The Autonomous AI-RU: Closing the RIC intelligence gap to the radio through the ruApp
Speakers: Laawanya Kishor (UGECO PRIVATE LIMITED), Rexy Geevarghese (Bell Canada)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/10120/
The Autonomous AI-RU: Closing the RIC intelligence gap to the radio through the ruApp

### From AI PC to AI Infrastructure: Platform Contracts for Scalable CPU–Accelerator Systems
Speakers: Dong Wei (ARM), Suraj Varma (Arm, Inc.)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9163/ | materials: https://developer.arm.com/documentation/den0151/latest/
As AI workloads expand from datacenter to edge and client systems, the industry is converging on a unified AI computing continuum. Scaling this continuum requires more than heterogeneous compute; it requires consistent, OS‑visible platform behavior across CPUs, accelerators, firmware, and system software. This session introduces platform contracts as the missing layer that enables reliable discovery, management, power coordination, security, and orchestration of CPU–accelerator systems. A platform contract defines how silicon capabilities are exposed and consumed consistently by firmware and operating systems. Drawing from PC‑BSA and SBSA, the talk highlights gaps in device enumeration, driver binding, and system readiness across environments. It presents a practical framework to improve interoperability and deployment at scale, and outlines how OCP and ecosystem partners can define consistent, open platform expectations for next‑generation AI systems.

### Bridging the AI Continuum: Trust, Orchestration, and Open Standards
Speakers: Steven Yates (Federant), Raúl Álvarez (Open Compute Project Foundation (OCP)), Vincent Nguyen (Hewlett Packard Enterprise), Dirk Blevins (Intel)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9274/ | pdf: https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/11196246 | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KjWdKJVZ1CM
AI inference is moving out of the hyperscale data center toward regional, colocation, telco, enterprise, and far-edge sites, forming an AI compute continuum that spans independently managed administrative and trust domains. This panel discusses where AI workloads will reside in the near term and how open infrastructures can contribute to trustworthiness across the continuum. We address three critical challenges: heterogeneous multi-provider orchestration without shared control planes, the hard problem of moving data and models into untrusted environments, and how hardware-anchored trust through Trusted Execution Environments, the Caliptra silicon root of trust, and OpenBMC can provide end-to-end attestation of AI workloads. Panel discusses open meta-operating systems for cross-domain orchestration and how the continuum can be rigorously validated on open standards-aligned platforms.

## AI Open DC: Power Distribution

### +/-400V / 800V On-Board Power Delivery Requirements
Speakers: James Sun (Microsoft), Sachin Madhusoodhanan (Google), LJ (Jian) Lu (Google), Shengwen Xu (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9529/
Data center High-power machine learning and AI applications require a power conversion from +/-400V and 800V to 50V or 12V by utilizing either unregulated ( fixed-ratio) or regulated modules. The OCP workstream aims to standardize these scalable solutions in Full, Half and Quarter Brick form factors which are required to achieve peak efficiencies of 98.5% and operate across a -40°C to 85°C range. To ensure reliability, specifications mandate strict EMC and safety compliance, advanced I2C/PMBus telemetry, and "blackbox" datalogging for real-time fault analysis. Standardizing our supplier approach will enable a scalable solution that meets rapidly growing demand. By establishing a focused set of requirements early on, suppliers can implement Hyperscaler’s desired products in advance, resulting in lower production costs and improved quality and supply assurance.

### From Principles to Practice: Implementing OCP LVDC Frameworks with a 600kW, 800VDC Sidecar Power System Design
Speakers: Guang Yang (Siemens), Tero Järveläinen (Skeleton Technologies)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9082/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/documents/ocp-specification-diablo-400-v0-7-0-final-pdf
AI data centers are trending towards higher DC voltages as GPU clusters drive faster load transients and higher rack densities. Beyond facility-level DC distribution, “DC sidecars” as specified by OCP Mt. Diablo ±400VDC are a promising alternative. Higher DC voltages demand strict protection principles, laid out by OCP DCF Power Distribution through its LVDC white paper and references, including a Siemens and NVIDIA white paper. In alignment with these frameworks, Siemens and Skeleton Technologies present a validated 600kW, 800VDC "DC sidecar" system design enabled by curved graphene-based high-power energy storage with DC-DC electronics to buffer GPU transients, improve ride-through, support grid compliance and reduce upstream oversizing. Utilizing modular high-efficiency 300kW AC-DC conversion, solid-state protection, insulation monitoring, and safe disconnect functions, OCP workflows are realized as a real-world system with exceptional performance.

### Defining End-to-End 800VDC System Performance Requirements
Speakers: Jared Huntington (NVIDIA), Mike Tu (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9557/
As 800 VDC emerges as a foundational architecture for future AI Factories, the industry must establish clear, measurable performance requirements to ensure interoperability, reliability, and compatibility across the power ecosystem. This session introduces a system-level framework for defining and validating end-to-end 800 VDC performance, spanning power sources, distribution systems, energy storage, and compute rack interfaces. Topics include DC system power quality, stability, oscillation control, power smoothing, energy storage integration, and fault ride-through. The session will also discuss the role of simulation and performance-based validation methodologies in ensuring compatibility between infrastructure equipment and high-density AI computing platforms. By establishing common performance criteria and pass/fail metrics, the industry can accelerate OEM development, reduce deployment risk, and enable scalable AI Factory growth.

### LVDC: Building an Open and Evolving Ecosystem
Speakers: Jason Adrian (Google), Jared Huntington (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9752/ | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kBmC4Om7UxA
The shift to native Low Voltage Direct Current (LVDC) data centers requires a comprehensive ecosystem, including power conversion via SSTs and TRUs, energy storage, power distribution, and 800V native cooling. Innovative 800-48V DC:DC power shelves also enable legacy 48V equipment to integrate into 800V environments. To drive industry adoption, Google, Nvidia, and Microsoft are sharing specifications, including the OCP SST and a new Universal LVDC Busway design for interoperable power distribution. These specifications and more will be contributed to the Open Compute Project to foster efficient, standardized infrastructure.

### Short Circuit Bolted Fault Analysis of LVDC Power Distribution Systems for Datacenter Uses
Speakers: Sarkis Juana (UL Solutions)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9142/
As low voltage DC (LVDC) power architectures gain interest for datacenter applications, understanding short-circuit behavior becomes increasingly important for equipment design, protection strategies, and safety evaluations. This study investigates bolted fault behavior in 800 VDC distribution systems through simulation of capacitor-driven fault transients. The analysis evaluates the influence of distributed bus capacitance, conductor size, cable length, temperature, and supercapacitor energy storage. Results demonstrate that fault severity is strongly influenced by the interaction of stored energy and loop impedance, with larger energy storage systems producing substantially higher fault currents and thermal stress. The work establishes a baseline framework for characterizing fault behavior in emerging LVDC datacenter architectures and identifies key parameters that influence protection device performance and system-level fault response.

### 800VDC Power Systems: Redefining Protection, Coordination & System Safety
Speakers: Manav Patel (Schneider Electric), Tony Landry (Schneider Electric)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9135/
AI-driven power densities in hyperscale data centers are driving a shift toward system-level design in 800VDC environments. This presentation explores the transition from device-level protection to coordinated architectures that address fault detection, interruption speed, and system stability at scale. It highlights DC-specific challenges—including lack of natural zero-crossing, arc persistence, and fault isolation complexity—requiring new approaches to selectivity, coordination, and validation. The discussion also outlines the need for integrated safety frameworks that extend beyond components to end-to-end system behavior, interoperability, and resilience under transient conditions. Finally, the role of digital validation is examined, leveraging simulation, continuous monitoring, and predictive analytics to enable pre-validated protection schemes and proactive fault mitigation in next-generation data centers.

### LVDC Architecture Fundamentals: How Topology, Protection, and Standardization Define DC Power Systems
Speakers: Brian Deckard (Siemens), Giel Van den Broeck (Direct Energy Partners)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9349/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/documents/dcf-power-distribution-lvdc-white-paper-version-1-0-final-pdf-1
The data center industry is rapidly transitioning from AC to DC distribution, with low-voltage DC emerging as a key enabler for high-density compute racks. While the OCP Power Distribution white paper and prior OCP EMEA presentations have addressed voltage levels, system topologies, and grounding, a broader architectural perspective is needed to connect these topics with standardization. This presentation relates design choices to established DC system classification principles, integrating IEC circuit classification with Current/OS protection zones and ODCA DC sectors into a unified view. We explore radial, ring, and mesh topologies in the context of protection and redundancy, and examine how isolation influences transitions between AC and DC systems and across voltage classes. Finally, we highlight how architecture, source type, and protection speed impact available short-circuit current, providing practical insight for scalable and resilient DC distribution.

### 2-Wire vs. 3-Wire at 800VDC: Grounding, Fault Behavior, and Protection for AI Data Centers
Speakers: Lizhi Wang (Siemens), Guang Yang (Siemens)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9121/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/documents/ocp-specification-diablo-400-v0-7-0-final-pdf
From OCP Mt. Diablo ±400VDC to DCF Power Distribution at 800VDC and beyond, 2-wire vs. 3-wire architectures strongly influence protection design. Building on OCP LVDC Power Distribution and Siemens-NVIDIA DC protection white papers, these trade-offs are explored in detail. The analysis incorporates less-studied parameters, such as distributed parasitic capacitance and insulation resistance, to evaluate common-mode voltage redistribution, weak first-fault detectability, and touch current. It further considers grounding types, short-circuit and overload faults, and protection devices. 3-wire-specific behaviors – midpoint displacement, half-bus imbalance, pole-to-midpoint faults, and residual energized paths after single-pole clearing – are examined. Rack-level energy storage is evaluated for fault current impact, fault ride-through, and load smoothing. Results provide actionable guidance for grounding, protection coordination, and storage sizing.

### Reliability, Availability, and Maintainability of High-Temperature Superconducting Power Distribution for High-Density AI Data Centers
Speakers: Bender Kutub (VEIR), Lee Prescott (Red Engineering Design)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9147/
As AI rack densities exceed 1 MW, conventional copper-based power distribution struggles to scale. HTS cable systems offer higher current capacity and lower resistive losses but introduce cryogenic cooling as a new mission-critical dependency. This paper presents a RAM analysis of VEIR's HTS power distribution across distributed redundant AC and DC architectures with centralized liquid nitrogen (LIN) cooling. Using fault tree analysis and IEEE 493-2007 data, the LIN system achieves 5-nines availability through N+1 redundancy; distributed redundant AC and DC architectures achieve Tier 4 (4-nines) availability. System availability is driven more by architecture and interdependencies than individual component statistics. Reducing repair times proved as impactful as equipment upgrades.

### Closing the Standards Gap for LVDC Power Distribution
Speakers: Lisa Grams (UL Solutions), Michael Jensen (UL Solutions)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9906/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/documents/dcf-power-distribution-lvdc-white-paper-version-1-0-final-pdf-1
AI-enabled computing is shaping today's energy frontier and transforming end-to-end power architectures for data centers. This dramatic shift is not limited to servers or racks: it spans the entire power chain from chip to the grid interconnection. The OCP Power Distribution sub-project was established with the goal to increase data center power density, enable next-generation computing, and transition data center infrastructure to Low Voltage Direct Current (≤1500VDC). The Codes and Standards workstream has been a key part of that sub-project to help enable interoperability and ensure compliance with safety and operational standards. This session will highlight the collaborative process done within the OCP Community to identify existing global system & product standards across UL, IEC, IEEE, etc., installation codes, gaps and standards in development for key power innovation areas, and interim guidance.

### Rethinking Power Safety: The Inherent Benefits of 800V Low Voltage DC Architectures
Speakers: Susmita Panda (Google), Satarupa Bal (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9486/
Low-voltage 800V DC architectures are rapidly becoming the standard for power-dense AI data centers, yet traditional safety frameworks often treat DC arc flash risks with extreme caution. In applications such as solar, BESS and traction power are notoriously persistent and destructive, driven by both large energy sources and inter-link power converters. However, next-generation data centers utilizing fault-limiting power converters fundamentally rewrite these rules. This presentation demonstrates that converter-dominated 800V DC systems can uniquely limit incident energy levels, eliminating the requirement of complex mitigation systems in the LVDC distribution. This approach streamlines PPE requirements at busway / rack level, making arc energy highly manageable. Advanced power converters, such as SST or DC UPS with their inherent fault limiting capability, creates this protective boundary.

### DC Arc Flash - Learnings from Practical Study and Test Results
Speakers: Tony Landry (Schneider Electric), Stuart Sheehan (Schneider Electric)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9139/ | materials: https://www.linkedin.com/posts/tony-landry-2698b5144_paper-arcflash-electricalsafetyworkshop-share-7427480931520638978-UsXT/?utm_source=share&utm_medium=member_desktop&rcm=ACoAACMEPjEBVjcIjhLl0d9FaDl6e7uEIj4hck0
As the industry aligns around 800 VDC for next generation AI data centers, concerns about risk mitigation are top of mind, particularly the challenge of managing arc flash risk. Is it possible today to conduct an arc flash analysis for an 800 VDC AI data center? What are the requirements? Can the existing methods be used? How accurate are they? What are the challenges? This presentation will cover the learnings from running a practical study, in collaboration with OCP member hyperscaler, on two different 800 VDC architectures, rack level (using a sidecar) and centralized, and will compare the study numbers with tests results.

### Designing Safe 800 VDC Systems: Protection, Fault Management, and Operations
Speakers: Mike Tu (NVIDIA), Xianyong Feng (Microsoft), Sai Somayajula (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9522/
As AI Factories scale toward gigawatt-class campuses and high-density computing, ensuring the safe deployment and operation of 800 VDC power systems becomes increasingly critical. This session explores the key design principles and operational practices required to enable reliable and safe 800 VDC infrastructure, including grounding strategies, protection architectures, fault management, and maintenance considerations. Attendees will learn how emerging technologies such as high-voltage interlocks, solid-state circuit breakers, advanced fault detection, and energy isolation methods can improve system safety while supporting scalable deployment. Through real-world deployment considerations and system-level design approaches, this session provides a practical roadmap for safely implementing next-generation 800VDC AI Factory infrastructure.

### What the Automotive Industry Can Teach Us About 800 VDC Data Centers
Speakers: Adam Morton (Flex)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9225/ | materials: https://flex.com/resources/press-kit-advancing-ai-factories-with-new-reference-designs-and-800-vdc-power-rack
As AI workloads drive rack densities beyond the practical limits of traditional 48-54 V architectures, the data center industry is accelerating toward 800 VDC power distribution. Unlike previous infrastructure transitions, adoption timelines are compressed by unprecedented demand for AI capacity, leaving operators little time to develop new technologies, safety practices, and operational models from the ground up. Fortunately, no one has to start from scratch. Other industries have already solved many of the challenges associated with the transition. The automotive sector has spent more than a decade deploying 800 VDC systems at scale, advancing power electronics, fault protection, thermal management, battery integration, insulation monitoring, and safety frameworks. This session examines which lessons can be directly applied to data centers, where adaptation is required, and how cross-industry expertise can help accelerate deployment while reducing technical and operational risk.

### Integrating Solid State Transformer to Data Center LVDC Ecosystem
Speakers: Satarupa Bal (Google), Mike Tu (NVIDIA), Ashish Upreti (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9808/
This talk is targeted to introduce the Solid State Transformer as a product under the umbrella of OCP DCF LVDC power distribution subproject. It represents the product standardization effort to enable AI/ML hyperscale data centers with low voltage direct current distribution technology direction.
