This workstream is looking to generate “OCP Solid State Transformer Specification” which details the power conversion product requirements to interface between data center Medium Voltage AC energy harvesting plane, and Low Voltage DC server systems. Besides, the community collaborators are also exploring potentials to collectively build test case libraries covering MV power quality from various types of energy sources, and LV load profile from different silicon rack types running training or inference workloads. SST contents presented in this talk will be focused around the mid-year specification release encompassing major system integration guidelines and details.

### Grid Interconnection Requirements for Large Loads and Path to Next-Generation Data Center Power Architectures
Speakers: Sai Somayajula (NVIDIA), Mike Tu (NVIDIA), Eric Mannarino (Equinix), Joshua Piruzza (Danovo Energy Solutions)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9422/ | materials: https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/designing-production-ready-battery-energy-storage-systems-for-ai-factories/
AI-driven compute is turning data centers into grid-scale loads, with hyperscale sites seeking 100-500+ MW interconnections and queues reaching hundreds of gigawatts. This panel examines what it takes to connect these facilities reliably, from emerging utility and ISO requirements to next-generation power architectures. Speakers will review grid code expectations for Large Computational Loads, including voltage regulation, frequency response, fault ride-through, telemetry, modeling, and operational transparency, with examples from ERCOT and NERC initiatives. The session links those requirements to facility design choices at the point of interconnection: medium-voltage switchgear, transformers, harmonic filtering, UPS topology, storage, on-site generation, and power-quality management. It also explores OCP's 400/800 V LVDC reference architecture and the PSPD and EMT models needed to represent AI data center behavior for stability, protection, and reliability studies.

### Grid Requirements and Mitigation options for Large Scale AI/ML Loads
Speakers: Ashish Upreti (Google), Mike Della Penna (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9749/ | pdf: https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/11366058 | materials: https://www.nerc.com/who-we-are/committees/reliability-and-security-technical-committee-rstc/subcommittees-working-groups-and-task-forces/large-loads-working-group-llwg
The expansion of Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML) has necessitated the development of large data center clusters, frequently exceeding hundreds of MW and scaling toward GW-level power consumption. These facilities exhibit dynamic load profiles that may challenge the stability of the Bulk Electrical System (BES). Notably, a 1,500 MW simultaneous load loss event in July 2024 in Northern Virginia underscored the critical need for more stringent interconnection requirements to prevent cascading grid failures. This presentation aims to captures the evolving utility requirements in different categories (Voltage Ride Through (VRT), Active Power Recovery (PFAPR),Active Power Ramp Limits, Forced Oscillation Limits) and options for mitigation (E.g., Rack Level, Facility Infrastructure level) that ensure mutual reliability between computing infrastructure and grid. This presentation will also document status on other industry efforts aligned on this topic.

### Using the Full Low Voltage Direct Current Range up to 1500V for Data Centers
Speakers: Laurens Mackay (DC Opportunities)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9563/ | materials: https://drive.google.com/file/d/1791GWKaE0WkeZfATpofKZDxPpwoWEQG0/view
In this presentation we will talk about the future landscape for a Direct-current Datacenter, moving forward from +-400VDC/800VDC to higher voltages like +-700VDC/1400VDC and areas will have to be addressed to make this a reality to be able to deliver more energy to AI racks keeping high efficiency.

### Solid-State Circuit Breaker (SSCB) and DC Fault Manager
Speakers: Ke Sun (Vertiv)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9987/
High-density 800 VDC architectures cannot be protected by applying legacy AC breaker assumptions to a faster fault domain. In a low-inductance DC bus, fault current can rise on a microsecond timescale; protection depends on coordinated sensing, selective isolation, overvoltage control, and validated re-energization logic. This session proposes an open “DC Fault Manager” reference architecture that separates localized solid-state circuit breaker action from centralized post-fault analysis, safety interlocks, telemetry correlation, and busway re-energization sequencing. The goal is not a proprietary implementation, but a community discussion on what OCP should standardize: fault- state messages, digital handshakes, SSCB behavior classes, sampling expectations, and safe reset sequences. Attendees will see how microsecond protection physics should translate into open interfaces so AI data centers can scale high-voltage DC safely without locking operators into closed power ecosystems.

## AI Open Data Center

### Open Systems for AI: Why Open Communities Are Essential for the Next Era of Computing Infrastructure
Speakers: Michael Sweeney (Phaidra.ai)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9725/ | materials: https://drive.google.com/file/d/1LFqQGAfyvOlJMVDmgY0EvJ3-pfNI4sWM/view?usp=sharing
As AI infrastructure scales toward gigawatt-class deployments, the data center industry faces growing challenges around power, cooling, interoperability, sustainability, and operational efficiency. Phaidra and leaders from NVIDIA and/or CoreWeave explore how OCP’s three strategic AI initiatives: Open Systems for AI, Open Cluster Designs for AI, and Open Data Centers for AI, unify OCP projects into a framework for AI infrastructure at global scale. The discussion examines why open communities and interoperable standards are increasingly essential across the AI data center stack, including liquid cooling, rack power, networking, telemetry, and operational management. Through real-world experiences, speakers explore how open operational data exchange serves as a foundation for “AI for AI infrastructure,” enabling intelligent optimization across complex AI environments while demonstrating how open communities like OCP drive more reliable, efficient, and sustainable AI operations at scale.

### Beyond the Build: Autonomous Robotics and the Future of Data Center Operations
Speakers: Shashank Gupta (Fleet Data Centers), Rob Lawson-Shanks (Molg), Evan Johnson (Microsoft), Maureen Tate (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8747/ | materials: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1t51XsWnQlZvqMEpeqiiIYAJEEtEm-QtV/edit?usp=drive_link&ouid=106651623269308081059&rtpof=true&sd=true
The data center industry's explosive growth—driven by AI and hyperscale workloads—has exposed a critical gap: the challenge of running facilities after they open. This panel assembles leading practitioners from hyperscale operators, autonomous robotics, and data center engineering to address Day Two operations head-on. Panelists examine how autonomous repair micro-factories, robotic rack handling, and AI-driven predictive maintenance are being deployed today—not as pilots, but at scale. Discussion moves from strategic framing into operational realities: safety in high-density AI environments, security and audit compliance, and machine-level repeatability for 99.9%+ uptime commitments. The session explores how open standards—championed by OCP's DC Automation Working Group—enable interoperability across robotic systems from multiple vendors. Panelists include Working Group co-leads Eric Xu (Meta) and Shashank Gupta (Fleet Data Center), alongside Rob Lawson-Shank (molg.ai

### Material Handling Automation for Next-Generation Datacenter Operations
Speakers: Rachel Soukup (Google), Ben Wong (Meta), Amir Porter (Microsoft)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9010/
As capacity demand grows datacenter operators are responding by developing novel facility designs and deploying more diverse rack configurations driving increasing heterogeneity in both building layouts and rack form factors. In this environment, material handling automation becomes critical to delivering capacity faster while maintaining operational safety.Datacenters(DC) present unique challenges for automated material handling systems, including space requirements, wide variety in rack types, unique network infrastructure, and stringent safety standards. To guide the industry toward standardization, we provide a holistic set of requirements for material handling automation equipment operating within DC. We address: space constraints, rack dimensions and weight envelopes, network requirements, and safety standards. Additionally, we present current AGV deployments at Datacenters, demonstrating applications in transporting both standard and larger, next-generation rack configurations.

### Automation-Ready Infrastructure Design: From Light to Compute
Speakers: Mike McKee-Hector (Microsoft), Dave Evans (Google), Ryan Olson (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9545/
The rapid scaling of AI infrastructure is driving unprecedented growth in compute density, optics complexity, and deployment velocity. Yet most rack-level operations still rely heavily on manual execution, creating growing challenges in operational consistency, contamination control, serviceability, and infrastructure reliability. Robotics and automation represent a major opportunity to improve deployment throughput, operational quality, and long-term scalability. However, broader adoption remains constrained by infrastructure architectures that were not designed for autonomous operations. Challenges span asset identification, cable routing, provisioning verification, service and repair workflows, and inconsistent mechanical interfaces across manufacturers. This presentation explores automation-ready design approaches and standardization opportunities that improve robotic interoperability while preserving efficient human operations.

### Supporting load smoothing, Fault Ride-through (FRT), and back up in 800 Vdc critical powertrains
Speakers: Stuart Sheehan (Schneider Electric)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9298/
Data center powertrains have a history of effectively integrating energy storage for the purposes of supporting power quality and back-up for critical loads. Traditionally optimized approaches are being challenged by new demands from both the load side and source side, leading to new considerations for AI power solutions. In the context of centralized LVDC architecture, these topics are being tackled in the Power Distribution sub-project; however, a broad range of architectures, prospective performance, and solutions are still in discussion. In particular, LVDC IT voltage bands are not harmonized and can significantly impact control and integration strategies. Join SE in discussion and sharing of our learnings in developing critical powertrains to meet requirements of both new 800 vdc IT equipment and power system stakeholder Fault Ride Thru (FRT) and smoothing requirements.

### 800 VDC AI Factories: From Architecture Alignment to Industry Deployment
Speakers: Mike Tu (NVIDIA), Jared Huntington (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9517/
As AI Factories scale toward gigawatt-class campuses and high-density computing, the industry is converging on 800 VDC as a key enabler for future power infrastructure. This session shares the latest progress in moving 800 VDC from concept to deployment, including cross-industry alignment among cloud service providers, OEMs, and regulatory organizations. Attendees will learn how NVIDIA’s DSX platform (AI Factory Architecture Reference) and ecosystem collaboration are defining a scalable, interoperable, and deployment-ready framework for AI Factory power distribution. Topics include architecture convergence, equipment readiness, system performance requirements, safety and regulatory progress, and the roadmap toward large-scale deployment. The session concludes with key lessons learned and next steps to accelerate industry adoption of 800 VDC infrastructure.

### Flexibility the Grid Can Count On: Turning Curtailable Load Into Interconnection Capacity
Speakers: Sean James (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8869/
The binding constraint on AI factory growth is no longer silicon or capital. It is the interconnection queue. Facing multi-year waits, much of the industry is defaulting to islanded behind-the-meter generation, mostly gas. That path is fast for a single project, but it strands capacity, returns nothing to the grid, and pushes costs onto everyone else. There is a better option, and the industry keeps describing it in terms the grid cannot act on: flexible load the grid can actually dispatch. Duke University's Nicholas Institute found that the 22 largest U.S. balancing authorities could absorb 76 to 126 GW of new load if it is curtailed for just 0.25 to 1.0 percent of annual uptime. Independent analysis shows the same lever lowers rates for all customers by raising utilization of infrastructure already in the ground. FERC's large-load interconnection rulemaking, recent PJM and SPP actions, and 2026 modeling of conditional-firm service all point the same way. The unsolved problem: planners still study new load as firm, and there is no shared way to make curtailable load deliverable, verifiable, and creditable at a point of interconnection. Averaged, voluntary flexibility does not move an interconnection study, and it does not beat the certainty of an island. This session frames that gap as an open-infrastructure problem. It proposes applying the model that standardized rack, power, and cooling to flexibility itself: common definitions for flexibility products, a reference architecture for the storage, controllable-load, and telemetry stack that delivers them, and validation an operator can take to an RTO. The goal is flexibility a grid planner will count, so constrained regions host more compute on existing infrastructure instead of retreating to islands.

### Can the Industry Sustain AI? The Water-Energy Nexus Debate
Speakers: David Quirk (DLB Associates Consulting Engineers), Michael Obradovitch (Ecolab), Megan Schneider (Accenture), Rob Coyle (Open Compute Project Foundation (OCP))
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9492/ | materials: https://www.nxtbook.com/nxtbooks/ashrae/ashraejournal_EDCXHT/index.php#/p/2 | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bo00eL6Zhdg
As AI infrastructure scales from traditional data centers to high-density AI factories, the industry is confronting a new set of interconnected challenges spanning power availability, cooling technology, water utilization, grid resiliency, and operational sustainability. The rapid growth of AI workloads is driving unprecedented demand for energy and accelerating adoption of liquid cooling, while increasing scrutiny around resource consumption and long-term infrastructure scalability. Bringing together leaders from engineering, operations, sustainability, and open infrastructure organizations (ASHRAE, OCP, and iMasons), the discussion will highlight emerging standards, operational best practices, and strategies for responsibly scaling AI capacity while balancing reliability, efficiency, resiliency, and resource stewardship. Attendees will gain practical insight into the tradeoffs, innovations, and collaborative efforts shaping next-generation AI infrastructure.

### Enabling Grid-Compliant Data Center Growth Through Advanced Energy Storage
Speakers: Jason Barmann (EPC Power)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9334/
As AI data centers expand, utilities are imposing stringent interconnection and power purchase agreement (PPA) requirements to maintain grid stability. These include limitations on ramp rates, mandatory ride-through capability during disturbances, and tighter controls on load variability. These requirements challenge the dynamic power profiles of AI compute. This presentation examines how energy storage systems can bridge this gap, enabling data centers to meet grid interconnect requirements without constraining growth. We will walk through what is becoming standard requirements for these use cases: fast-response controls, configurable ramp-rate smoothing, voltage and frequency ride-through support, and seamless integration with on-site generation. Through real-world deployment examples and model validation testing, we demonstrate how storage can act as a flexible buffer between the grid and the data center load, reducing interconnection barriers and accelerating project timelines.

### The New Focus on Energy Storage Systems Safety
Speakers: Daniel Lambert (ZincFive, Inc.), Shrikanth Venkateshappa (Axiom)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8778/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/community/energy-storage-systems
During this session we will share the progress toward developing a concensus "best practice" guideline for the safety regulations that apply to Energy Storage Systems (ESS) installed in data centers globally. With contributions from hyperscalers, ESS manufacturers, standards development organizations, and liability insurance carriers (among others), everyone in the industry has an opportunity to participate. We will share the recently published Whitepaper on "Requirements for Energy Storage Systems Used in Data Centers", and show the progress of the Energy Storage Solutions Subproject, the Workstreams that are being developed, and how you can contribute to the progress being made. If you are involved in ESS used in data centers in any way, we need your participations!

### The Compliance Gap: ESS Safety in the Age of AI Infrastructure
Speakers: Shrikanth Venkateshappa (Axiom), Mauro Leuce (Digital Realty), Mike Pagel (Americase)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8958/ | materials: https://drive.google.com/file/d/1b44u3jXQgeVGbmz5_p1ctzsXrxZKBzix/view?usp=sharing
As AI infrastructure drives data center power densities higher, Energy Storage Systems - from rack-level BBUs to campus-scale BESS - are being deployed at unprecedented scale. Yet the regulatory frameworks governing how these systems are stored, transported, installed, and decommissioned remain fragmented and poorly understood by most practitioners.In January 2026, the OCP Ready Facilities Lead working group published Requirements for Energy Storage Systems Used in Data Centers - the most comprehensive ESS safety guidance the OCP community has produced. This panel brings together three of its authors: a global hazmat compliance expert, the OCP Ready Facilities Work Stream Lead, and a senior engineering standards leader from one of the world's largest colocation operators. The session translates that guidance into practical action for operators, OEMs, and facilities engineers worldwide.

### Superconducting Power Distribution for AI Data Centers: Quantified Benefits and Open Challenges Applied to a 250 MW Reference Architecture
Speakers: Frances Bosman (VEIR), Peter Gross (PMG Associates Consulting and Advisory), Chris Thompson (Vertiv)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9621/ | materials: https://veir.com/solutions
AI factory power delivery is approaching a constraint that copper cannot resolve. At 250 MW IT, medium-voltage feeder counts, low-voltage busway congestion, and routing corridors become first-order design constraints. This session presents a quantitative analysis of superconducting power distribution applied to a 250 MW IT reference architecture, evaluating two scenarios: direct cable substitution within the existing layout, and an architectural reorganization enabled by near-zero-loss LV feeders. Results include measured reductions in conductor volume, civil scope, installation labor, and overhead routing space, alongside a new finding: grey space can be decoupled from white space, enabling more compact and scalable layouts. Deployment barriers such as standards gaps are addressed directly. The session concludes with a call to action for the OCP community to initiate the open interface definitions and protection scheme standards required for commercial deployment.

### OCP Modular Technology Cooling Workstream - 2026 Update
Speakers: Dariusz Raczkowski (Catalyst Commissioning Group LLC), Sara Hitt (Vertiv), Jerrod Buterbaugh (AMD), Rajat Bhagat (Arcadis)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9524/
Scaling Cooling for AI Factories: Latest Updates from the OCP Modular Technology Cooling Systems (TCS) Workstream Since the last OCP Global Summit, the OCP Modular Technology Cooling Systems (TCS) Workstream has expanded its guidance to address the rapidly evolving requirements of AI Factories, including higher rack densities, accelerated deployment schedules, and increased reliability expectations. This session highlights the latest updates to the Modular TCS @ Cloud Scale White Paper, including guidance on modular design and delivery, 25-micron filtration, air removal and degassing, fluid performance management, thermal resilience, offsite pre-commissioning. How BIM-enabled digital engineering, DfMA methodologies, and standardized modular architectures can reduce schedule risk while improving quality, scalability, and sustainability. Attendees will gain practical insights and a preview of future workstream initiatives supporting liquid cooling at cloud scale.

### From Digital Twin to Production Readiness: A Closed-Loop Approach to AI Factory Deployment at Scale
Speakers: John Hsia (Pegatron Corporation)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9410/
AI factory validation often spans disconnected design, test, and deployment processes, making it difficult to translate digital twin insights into production readiness for deployment at scale. This session presents a closed-loop approach that connects digital twin creation, simulation, and full-load factory validation within one manufacturing environment. In Pegatron’s L11 Thermal Lab, CFD, power, and liquid-cooling simulations are used to generate DSX-aligned SimReady assets and evaluate behavior across multiple IT load conditions, including scenarios used to prepare for larger deployments. In the adjacent L12 Burn-In Room, each rack undergoes 100% TDP stress testing, with physical measurements cross-validated against the same digital twin before shipment. This workflow helps reduce deployment risk, strengthen production readiness, and create a more consistent baseline for downstream operations as AI factory infrastructure scales.

### Forged from Scratch: A DEC/HAC Playbook for Humidification-Off and Right-Sized DP Operation
Speakers: Yusuke Tatsumi (Actapio, Inc.), Osamu Kurino (Actapio, Inc.)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9237/
Improving PUE and WUE does not require heavy CapEx; conservative commissioning defaults leave measurable savings. This session shares a field-tested DCF playbook from a live OCP-server deployment optimizing Direct Evaporative Cooling (DEC) with Hot Aisle Containment (HAC). We evaluated ASHRAE low-humidity operation under ESD guardrails by verifying floor conductivity with a localized low-RH enclosure, desiccant, and ESD test kits before disabling active DEC humidification. This delivered a >30% YoY reduction in DEC water use while remaining within ASHRAE guidelines, with no observed increase in IT hardware-fault rates versus baseline over 6 months. With HAC leakage sealed, we characterized fan power versus differential pressure (DP) and right-sized DP under recirculation guardrails, achieving >60% lower DEC power. We also share filter telemetry, event-based trend analysis, and guardrail logic so DCF operators can adapt the playbook across OCP and 19-inch facilities.

### Challenges for High Density Workloads in Modular Data Center
Speakers: Daniel Robbins (RakworX, Inc)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9683/
AI and HPC workloads are pushing rack densities from 10–15 kW toward 100+ kW, forcing a rethink of modular data center design. This presentation examines the core challenges of deploying high-density compute within prefabricated, space-constrained modules. We address thermal management as air cooling reaches its limits and direct liquid and immersion cooling become mandatory — and the TCS pipe distribution, manifolds, and CDU placement this demands. We tackle power delivery at density: medium-voltage intake, busway sizing, and redundancy within a compact footprint. We explore structural and weight loads from liquid systems, the integration of energy storage for transient AI power spikes, and the commissioning complexity of pre-fabricated builds. Finally, we consider how modular construction and OCP-aligned standards can de-risk these deployments. Attendees will gain practical insight into delivering reliable, sustainable high-density capacity at speed.

### Evaluating Heat Rejection System Configurations for AI Data Centers: A Multi-Metric Analysis
Speakers: Bahareh Eslami (NVIDIA), Doug Asay (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9632/
As AI data centers push thermal densities higher, Heat Rejection System (HRS) architecture choices — from direct dry cooler feeds to intermediate heat exchanger loops and chiller-based trim cooling — carry significant design implications for both infrastructure and operations. The placement and configuration of HRS components directly influence piping complexity, operational overhead, and long-term facility performance. This work presents a comparative analysis of two primary facility architectures each evaluated across multiple HRS configurations and assessed against four key metrics: Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE), Water Usage Effectiveness (WUE), Total Cost of Ownership (TCO), and cooling density per unit footprint. By examining these tradeoffs together, this study offers data center designers and operators a more complete framework for HRS design decisions in high-density AI workloads. Contributors: Bahareh Eslami, Doug Assay, Raha Kalantarpour

### Next-Generation ML Infrastructure: The Open DC Architecture
Speakers: Ayan Majumdar (Google), Paul Artman (AMD), Mike Edie (Meta), Maureen Tate (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9487/
Rapid ML advancement requires evolving data center infrastructure. Google, Meta, NVIDIA, and AMD established the Open DC Specification to standardize high-density server halls for 2026–2028 & beyond, establishing a universal AI datacenter blueprint . Key technical focuses include: Power: Managing power densities from 125 kW/ft to 350 kW/ft with unified interfaces for redundancy. Physical: Architectural guidelines for liquid-cooled environments, specifying >70-inch rack depths and >80-inch cold aisles. Thermal: Scaling liquid cooling for high-TDP accelerators. This initiative empowers the ecosystem to efficiently deploy mega-scale AI factories through collaborative, open-source standards.

### Hardware Mitigations for AI/ML Workloads Power Fluctuation
Speakers: Vijay Boovaragavan (Google), Xiong Li (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9519/
Synchronized power fluctuations caused by ML workloads can cause damage to data centers or even utility power infrastructure if not properly managed. Energy storage solutions are critical to managing power fluctuations. Interim software based solutions exist but come with performance trade-offs. End-to-end hardware solutions from chip to facility level are needed to “smooth out” the power profile presented to utility or onsite generators. In this talk, we will explain the potential challenges and impacts from power fluctuation. Google’s view of high-level frameworks to address the challenges. An overview of different energy storage technologies and their suitability for power fluctuation smoothing. Call for actions for solution ecosystem (supercapacitor and battery based energy storage solutions at different integration level) enablement and enhancement.

### Large-Scale Deployment of 800Gb/s Active Electrical Cables (AEC) in Meta Data Centers for AI Applications
Speakers: Jingyu Yu (Meta), Abhijit Chakravarty (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8977/
Scaling AI training and inference clusters demands interconnects with higher bandwidth, lower latency, and improved power efficiency. This paper presents Meta's qualification, validation, and deployment methodology for 800 Gb/s Active Electrical Cables (AECs) operating at 112 Gb/s in large-scale GPU clusters. A framework spanning component characterization, environmental reliability, multi-vendor interoperability, system-level traffic validation, and manufacturing readiness was executed across multiple suppliers and production sites. Validation tests in laboratory and AI cluster environments confirmed link stability, packet integrity, latency performance, firmware compatibility, and operational robustness. The deployment program resolved signal integrity, manufacturing consistency, firmware interoperability, and reliability challenges, enabling volume production adoption of multi-sourced 800G AECs as a power- and cost-efficient short-reach interconnect for hyperscale AI infrastructure.

### Will to Power: Technoeconomic comparison of DC- and AC-coupled solar microgrids,  natural gas, and grid AI data center power
Speakers: Alex Newkirk (Berkeley National Lab)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9241/ | materials: https://www.researchsquare.com/article/rs-8272920/v1
Silicon you can't power is a paperweight. The AI buildout is colliding with delayed grid connection and equipment waitlists; with idle generation draining, developers bring their own power. The fastest paths are gas and PV+BESS; the latter are born DC. DC power avoids inversion AC requires. The questions are: how fast, how much, where, at what scale? We compare four ways to power AI data centers: DC- or AC-coupled PV+BESS, islanded gas, and grid connection. Our open-source framework models each across U.S. locations and scales at identical reliability. All-DC distribution cuts lifetime energy costs 18% (9¢/kWh) vs. equivalent AC. Though rarely cheapest per kWh, a 24-month PV+BESS build is lowest-cost in 85% of locations once foregone compute is valued at GPU rental rates (55% under accelerated gas timelines). We present the lowest-cost architecture by location, the design choices driving the DC advantage, semi-islanded operation, and the GPU price and timelines at which it flips.

### Path to the Megawatt Rack: A Grid-Aware, Symmetric High-Power UPS for AI Data Centers
Speakers: Tyler Andrews (Echion Technologies), Drew Schaller (Proper Voltage)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9802/ | materials: https://drive.google.com/file/d/1S9RJqoEbdHRFaPeIDlMtdGMfe30qeoQO/view?usp=drive_link | pdf: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2508.14318v1
AI training & inference clusters impose a power profile unlike any prior data-center load: synchronized GPUs drive megawatt-scale swings within milliseconds, stressing both upstream grid infrastructure and the UPS systems meant to protect the IT load. Conventional UPS energy storage, optimized for rare, one-directional backup events, is poorly matched to the continuous, symmetric, high-rate charge and discharge these loads demand. Vault X is a UPS energy-storage system that integrates Echion’s XNO® niobium-anode cells into Proper Voltage’s Vault platform. This XNO® battery technology delivers symmetric high-C-rate operation, ultra-long cycle life, and high safety, enabling a UPS that not only rides through outages but actively buffers AI load volatility and supports grid-facing services. Because Vault X slots into Proper Voltage’s existing OEM-integrated architecture, it offers a de-risked, near-term path to deployment for UPS OEMs and hyperscale operators.

### The Future of Fuel Cells in Datacenters - High Temperature Fuel Cells.
Speakers: Afshin Majd (Microsoft), Priya Chhiba (Google), Alberto Ravagni (Net Zero Innovation Hub)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9205/
High-temperature fuel cell technology is being deployed at GW scale in data centers as an efficient, low-emission source of prime power. As modular, dispatchable assets, these systems support flexible behind-the-meter (BTM) strategies for AI-scale campuses. They offer high efficiency, low emissions, and strong integration with carbon capture and heat recovery, enabling substantial carbon reduction (up to 95%) and system efficiencies above 90% when combined with heat recovery plans. A key advantage of this technology is compatibility with emerging DC systems, including 800V DC architectures, reducing conversion losses and simplifying electrical design. This technology also supports hydrogen production through electrolysis as well as fuel-based long-duration energy storage systems. This presentation focuses on chemistry evaluation, technology adoption readiness, application mapping, and system integration for scalable, resilient, lower-carbon data center infrastructure.

### COMMISSIONING LIQUID COOLED DATA CENTERS WITH COMPUTING EQUIPMENT FOR OPERATIONAL READINESS
Speakers: Vahideh Radmard (NVIDIA), John Herboth (Glumac), Sara Hitt (Vertiv), Eric Kumar (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9433/ | materials: https://drive.google.com/file/d/1l5teu7jYA43u5yjm3pnJhEmNtHMPM98B/view?usp=sharing
As data centers increasingly support high-density AI and advanced compute deployments, commissioning practices must evolve to better reflect real operating conditions. This white paper explores the role of commissioning with actual compute load as a step following Level 5b performance verification and validation. While traditional load-bank testing remains important, actual compute load can provide additional insight into how infrastructure performs under realistic electrical, thermal, and operational conditions. The paper introduces the concept of using live compute environments as part of the later-stage commissioning process to support system validation, operational readiness, and confidence before production handoff. It highlights why this approach is becoming more relevant for modern data centers and frames actual compute-load commissioning as a complementary method for reducing risk and improving reliability in high-density environments before full operation.

### The AI Factory Digital Twin: Optimizing Tokens per Watt Across the Full Stack
Speakers: Kourosh Nemati (NVIDIA), Sherman Ikemoto (Cadence), Matthew Käufeler (Cadence), Usha Kodali (NVIDIA)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9693/
Liquid cooling has moved from the edge case to the design center, reshaping the thermal, hydraulic, and mechanical envelope of the entire facility. The AI factory has transformed the data center from a cost center into a profit center, where every Watt matters. Operators and designers must optimize for Tokens/W across a tightly coupled stack of rack, row, and facility. These layers do not operate independently, as architectures evolve and workloads shift, this optimization must be continuous. This is where DT becomes essential. A live, sensor- and workflow-driven DT, combining CFD simulation with AI, enables design teams to validate liquid cooling architectures before commissioning, predict thermal and hydraulic behavior as rack densities increase, and give operators the tools to tune Tokens/W continuously. Here, through real-world examples, we show how a DT has become a foundational tool for AI factories to deliver throughput, uptime, and return.

## Cooling Environments

### Cooling Environments Kick-off and Lightning Talks
Speakers: Steve Mills (META)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9166/
These Lightning Talks will provide an overview of the Cooling Environments Project plus rapid updates on some of the work the project is doing. Cooling Environments Project Overview and Update — Steve Mills Cooling Technologies Testing Workstream & Data Center Cooling Collaborative (DC Cool) — Donghun Kim, LBNL Cooling Environments – ASHRAE TC9.9 & EE HPC WG Collaboration Updates — Matt Koukl Industry Liaison Team Update — Kelley Mullick Accelerating Liquid Cooling Interoperability: AI-Driven Redfish Schema Development, Mock-Ups, and OCP Profile Validation — Mick Jones, Vertiv Community Update: Total Cost of Ownership Workstream — Eduard Roytman, Microsoft Experimental Framework for Determining Coolant Flow Velocity Limits in Liquid-Cooled AI Factory TCS Loops — Qusai Soud, Nvidia

### What Shapes CDU Performance? A Comparative Study of Design Choices for 2.3 MW Systems
Speakers: Jake How (Gamma Technologies)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9663/ | materials: https://www.gtisoft.com/blog-post/modeling-the-google-deschutes-cdu-in-gt-suite-a-blueprint-for-liquid-cooling-success/
What does it take to hit a ~2.3 MW CDU target—and what are the real tradeoffs behind the design? In this OCP CDU workstream collaboration between Gamma Technologies, Stulz, and Alfa Laval, we use the Deschutes v5 architecture as a shared benchmark to systematically explore alternative design paths. Using validated, physics-based simulation alongside vendor-informed component models, we compare how choices in heat exchangers, pumps, flow rates, fluids, and piping strategies shape performance. Results reveal quantified impacts on cooling capacity, ATD, pressure drop, pump energy, footprint, serviceability, and relative cost. This session delivers actionable, data-driven insights to help the OCP community make smarter, system-level CDU design decisions.

### Cooling Distribution Unit Sub-Project Overview and Updates
Speakers: Pardeep Shahi (NVIDIA), Hussam Kabbani (Google)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9471/
This presentation will highlight the ongoing collaborative efforts of the community since the commencement of the project. It will provide an overview of the work completed to date and outline the proposed test matrix and process alignment for CDU self-validation by CDU vendors supporting data center applications.-

### Door Heat Exchanger Sub-Project Updates and Lightning Talks
Speakers: Rajesh Kasukurthy (AMD), Dominik Sporna (Valeo), Joe Thomas (STULZ), Mohamad Hnayno (OVHcloud)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9340/
This Lightning Talk will provide an overview of the Door Heat Exchanger Sub-Project, followed by rapid updates on its specifications and technical workstreams. Speakers Door Heat Exchanger Sub-Project Overview and Update — Jabari George, LGTS Scaling Aluminum Heat Exchangers in Data Centers: Performance, Corrosion, and PUE Validation Updates — Mohamad Hnayno, OVHcloud; Dominik Sporna, Valeo Mega Sidecar AALC to Enable AI Hardware in Legacy Data Center Infrastructure — Rajesh Kasukurthy, AMD; Adrian Zebrowski, Meta OCP Base Specification – Door Heat Exchanger — Vincent Chan, STULZ; Ashok Yadav, Vertiv

### Building a Scalable Waste Heat Ecosystem: Datacenter Waste Heat, Offtakers, and Market Platforms
Speakers: Maria Viitaniemi (Microsoft)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9170/
Turning waste heat into a scalable resource is becoming a key enabler of sustainable datacenter growth. As capacity expands, so does the volume of recoverable heat, coinciding with increased regulatory and societal pressure to put that energy to use. To date, most efforts focus on district heating, yet in many regions this path is constrained by infrastructure limitations. In response, we are exploring a broader set of waste heat recovery (WHR) offtakers. This includes technologies aligned with local needs, such as water treatment or water capture from air in water-scarce regions. This work introduces a structured framework for selecting heat offtakers, incorporating techno-economic feasibility and community value. We illustrate its use for representative locations and diverse offtaker types, demonstrating how heat supply can be optimally matched with demand. In parallel, we highlight contributions toward a marketplace and standardized framework to enable more scalable WHR deployment.

### Heat Reuse Sub-Project Overview and Updates + Economics Tool Contribution
Speakers: Jaime Comella (DayOne), Jack Kolar (Dataquarium), Gemma Reeves (Alfa Laval), Ahliana Byrd (Ascentra Innovations)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/10153/
Join the Heat Reuse Subproject team for a comprehensive update on our latest cross-workstream advancements aimed at accelerating waste heat utilization in the data center industry. This session provides the audience with actionable, real-world frameworks. We will start with the updates on Reference Designs workstream, focusing on heat reuse for freshwater production and facility water conservation, alongside key insights gathered from interviews with operational real-world cases. After that, Policies/Outreach/Liaison workstream will show the latest on policies, an AI-powered policy finder tool, news on the Heat Exchange Platform and a "heat reuse ready" checklist designed to streamline regulatory compliance and outreach. We will continue introducing the launch of a new workstream and its roadmap to achieve a base specification for Heat reuse modules. We will conclude with a deep dive into the Economics workstream, showcasing our latest contribution: Data Center Heat Reuse Economics tool, to help the community build financial cases for data center heat reuse infrastructure.

### Waste Heat Recovery Pathways for AI Data Centers: Emerging Opportunities, Decarbonization Potential, and Future Perspectives
Speakers: Yingbo Zhang (UC Santa Barbara)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9278/
The AI revolution is generating unprecedented volumes of high-quality waste heat, creating a significant opportunity to improve AI data center sustainability. This study addresses a critical knowledge gap by systematically reviewing real-world deployments and scientific research on data center waste heat utilization. We identify various recovery pathways, assess enabling factors and implementation barriers, and quantify the potential energy and carbon impacts. Direct air capture demonstrates substantial decarbonization potential, achieving carbon reductions of up to 233.59 g CO₂ per MJ of waste heat reused. The annual decarbonization potential of a 30 MW data center is equivalent to the carbon sequestration of about 3.65 million urban trees. This study provides a foundation for future research and informs policymakers and stakeholders on sustainable AI data center development.

### Managing Liquid Cooling At-Scale: Tooling, Telemetry, and Reliability for High-Density AI Racks
Speakers: Zach Miller (Meta), Kalpak Dhake (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9757/
As liquid-cooled AI racks scale to tens of thousands of units across multiple hardware generations and vendors, fleet operators face challenges that traditional datacenter management never addressed: multi-vendor firmware divergence, failure modes unique to coolant systems, and telemetry implemented via I2C, BMC interfaces, and cross-rack communication. We share the operational discipline we've developed for managing liquid cooling hardware at scale: machine-readable tooling that provide detection, diagnosis, and repair across hardware generations; a telemetry pipeline that translates raw low-level Modbus/I2C registers into fleet-level monitoring; and reliability lessons from production incidents including liquid cooling system failures, leaks, environmental false positives, and firmware misconfigurations. Every incident closes the loop back into automated detection. Attendees leave with reusable patterns for scaling liquid cooling operations.

### Machine-Verifiable CDU Safety Procedures: A Worked Extraction from OCP Project Deschutes
Speakers: Nauman Rafique (Visum AI), Marco Pritoni (Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9766/ | materials: https://visum.ai/ai-factories-are-scaling-faster-than-the-people-who-run-them/
A coolant distribution unit in a liquid-cooled AI factory sits over energized busways, and its procedures govern coolant isolation, fault response, and lockout-tagout on the variable-frequency drive. Those procedures live as vendor PDFs, disconnected from the drawings, manuals, and standards that ground them. We present a machine-verifiable safety layer: each procedure step is bound to the equipment, interlocks, and isolation points it acts on, so a coolant-leak procedure missing an isolation point, or a lockout step naming no disconnect, is caught automatically. We demonstrate it on an OCP Project Deschutes CDU (v0.8.0, Google, OCP 2025) with Marco Pritoni at Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory: four isolation points across fluid and electrical circuits, the VFD main disconnect recorded as both a maintenance step and an OSHA 1910.147 lockout-tagout step, and a coolant-leak response with automated and manual steps typed separately. Offered to the Brick Consortium.

### Air vs. Liquid: Where Does Air Cooling Still Win for General Compute?
Speakers: Jayesh Shah (Microsoft), Stewart Nguyen (Lenovo)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8621/
With CPU power moving into the high hundreds of watts & memory & I/O density continuing to rise, the industry is accelerating toward liquid cooling. The key question is not whether liquid will be required, but where air cooling can still scale for general-purpose workloads. We examine the practical operating envelope of air cooling & how system-level design choices, such-as: chassis geometry, heatsink scaling, & DDR6-era component placement, can extend its applicability. Rather than assuming an immediate transition to liquid, we explore how mechanical & thermal design can push air cooling beyond commonly assumed limits. We compare air & liquid cooling across performance, operational complexity, & total cost to provide a clear decision framework. Air where it works, and liquid where physics demands it. As platforms evolve, this perspective helps inform standards, system architecture, & infrastructure planning, ensuring air cooling remains a scalable option alongside liquid cooling.

### Know Before You Flow — PG25 Mixing Thresholds for DLC
Speakers: Sung A Kim (bp Castrol), Jake Davis (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9003/
As direct liquid cooling proliferates across hyperscale and colocation environments, the risk of inadvertent mixing between PG25 coolant chemistries — particularly OAT and IAT glycol families — has emerged as a critical operational challenge. This presentation shares findings from a multi-organization Design of Experiment (DOE) examining coolant compatibility across real-world pilot loops hosted at major hyperscaler facilities, with external validation through accredited laboratory testing. Bringing together fluid chemists, data center operators, and infrastructure OEMs, the initiative is developing practical mixing thresholds, remediation protocols, and sampling standards to inform both operator SLAs and future industry guidance.

### Maximizing Cooling Performance in Data Centers by Optimizing Fluid Velocity Limits
Speakers: Matthew Schantz (Gates Corporation), William Grant (Gates Corporation)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8779/ | materials: https://codes.iapmo.org/docs/2015/UPC/articles/The%20Importance%20of%20Velocity%20in%20a%20Piping%20System.pdf
As data center cooling demands increase, fluid velocity is emerging as a key lever for improving heat transfer capacity in liquid cooling systems. This work evaluates the impact of elevated flow velocity on peroxide-cured EPDM hose assemblies used in coolant distribution, including hoses up to 2-inch inside diameter operating with PG25 coolant. Through analytical modeling and empirical testing, the study examines how increased velocity influences thermal performance, pressure drop, and system constraints. Key considerations such as material durability, flow-induced vibration, and long-term reliability are assessed to define safe operating limits. The whitepaper/results provide a framework for engineers and system designers to extend velocity thresholds beyond current conventions while maintaining system integrity. These findings enable improved thermal management, optimized component selection, and increased system capacity, supporting continued scaling of liquid cooling architectures.

### Fluid Flush/Fill Cart for Pre-Deployment Conditioning of Liquid-Cooled IT Systems
Speakers: Himanshu Modi (NVIDIA), Kevin Joseph (Meta)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9203/
This Specification establishes the Base Specification layer for a portable Fluid Flush/Fill Cart used to flush, fill, condition, sample, and recover working fluid in the secondary liquid loops of OCP-compatible Coolant Distribution Units (CDUs), row manifolds, rack manifolds, and direct-to-chip cold plates. The primary working fluid envelope is 25% propylene glycol / water (PG25) per the Technology Cooling System (TCS) loop conventions established in OCP Cooling Environments documentation

### Rack-Scale Weight Reduction and Reliability Validation for Liquid-Cooled Server Systems Using Lightweight Materials
Speakers: Jeff CA Chen (Wiwynn Corporation), Shawn Ma (Wiwynn Corporation)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/9103/
As server compute density continues to increase, liquid cooling significantly raises rack weight, creating challenges for floor loading, transportation, and structural integrity. With next-generation AI server racks expected to exceed 6000 lb, infrastructure limits are becoming a critical constraint, making weight reduction a key design priority. This study identifies major liquid-cooled components contributing to system weight and investigates material substitution strategies for weight reduction. The feasibility of replacing metallic components with lightweight engineering plastics is evaluated while maintaining thermal performance and mechanical strength. Integrated reliability testing is also conducted to validate the proposed design. This work provides a practical framework for developing next-generation lightweight liquid-cooled racks that support higher compute density while addressing infrastructure limitations, with overall rack weight reduction expected to exceed 30%.

### Liquid Cooling Market Update: Industry Dynamics, Adoption Signals, and the Road to Scale
Speakers: Rolf Brink (Promersion)
page: https://2026ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/8555/
Liquid cooling has moved from a specialized topic to a strategic requirement for high-density compute, AI infrastructure, and next-generation data center design. This session provides an analytical update on the evolving liquid cooling landscape, with a focus on market dynamics, adoption drivers, supplier positioning, technology segmentation, and the shifting balance between cold plate, immersion and hybrid approaches. The session will examine how AI, rack density, energy constraints, supply chain capacity, OEM roadmaps, end-user risk perception, and facility readiness are reshaping the market. It will also look beyond headline growth claims to assess what is actually changing, which signals matter, and where adoption friction remains. Attendees will gain a grounded view of how the ecosystem is evolving, what this means for strategic positioning, and how organizations should interpret current market movement.
